LADIES' LETTER

Sly DEAR NELLIE. If the home milliner's ambitions lead her to attempt the copying of the elaborate—not to say eccentric—headgear to be seen in the smartest and most fashionable shops, she is likely to meet with scant success, for these wonderful colour combina- tions and marvellous mixtures of flowers and feathers, ribbons and laces, need a skilled hand to make them at all beautiful. But there are, never- theless, some pretty yet simple styles in vogue which are quite within the powers of an amateur. Suppose the shape chosen is of coarse oatmeal- coloured (this is newer than the brighter "sun- burnt " tint) straw, and that it has a fairly broad brim turned up at the back and a moderate crown; it must first be edged with a narrow band of black

velvet, then round the top of the crown should be arranged a twist of the same, and below this, resting on the brim, a light drapery of lace just the shade of the straw should form a background for a wreath of green cherry leaves with a small cluster of deep red berries here and there. At the back of the crown the twist of black velvet ribbon must be finished by a smart how of the velvet and some wired loops of lace, in the midst of which there should stand erect a small bunch of cherries, while under the brim, resting on the hair—or rather the indispensable comb—a bunch of the fruit should be half con. cealed in a full bow of lace. A hat of precisely similar shape and straw would look well, too, ii the brim were bound with green velvet and a tkick wreath of shaded ivy put round the crown with a big, wide bow of green glacti silk at the back and a cacheleigue of pink roses under the brim.

For evening wear cloth skirts, in cream, pink, yellow, and blue, are worn with blouses of silk in the same colouring, but almost covered with hand- some applique; or guipute lace, or with draperies of embroidery, tulle, or chiffon. Lace, indeed, plays a most important part in summer attire, and there are shaped "precernents" for bodices, blouses, and mantles, also garland trimmings, which can be separated in sprays or separate motifs for arranging on pleats or bands, or to powder the material like a raised pattern. One of the blouses can be of bright blue linen, made in Russian style, fastening down one side with a pleat and little frill, and the other of either red soft silk or flannelette, made with a wide turn- down collar and three box-pleats in front, the collar and gauntlet cuffs being edged with white embroidery if the blouse is of flannelette, but otherwise with very narrow frills of the silk. The reefer coat is, of course, just a plain, straight, double-breasted shape, but the present fashion of adding a large collar ot grass-cloth or embroidered muslin gives the simple jacket a very smart armearance.

Very smart frocks can be made of China silk striped with insertions of lace throughout, or of muslin similarly trimmed, and worn over a silk slip. Sashes are folded rather narrow, but the bows at the back are large and the ends long. Belts of narrow satin ribbon or velvet, finished In front with a clasp, buckle, or small bow, are quite as fashionable for girls of all ages from ten upwards as the broader sashes. As to hats, for school. wear the plain wide-brimmed " sailor " shape with band add bow of rather broad glace ribbon is nicest, while to wear with a party frock there is nothing prettier than a shady hat of pure white chip, trimmed with ruches of white gauze and either ostrich tips or wings. At present the feather boa is conspicuously fashionable, and the plain ones reach only to the waist, while shorter ones, with four to six tails, are very elegant. Elaborate boas of chiffon or gauze, with all the quillings edged bouillonnees of chiffon, will be worn later on, and the extremely full boa of lace is shown in black and white and black and ru, while fussy bows of tulle are inter- Inized with the most fragile lace. For matronly wearers, very handsome capes are of sequin-embroidered net in jet and steel, mounted on mery or glace silk, and beautifully finished with pleatings of gauze and grelots and fringes inter- mixed. Sac coats accompany many of the tailor- made morning costumes, and open-fronted coats have very short, tightly-fitted basques, and the fashion is to employ braiding to cover the bosom darts and curved back seams, while revers are faced with white or biscuit cloth, braided at the edge, or with silk, outlined with applique designs in braid, jet, or lace. Your loving JENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Her Majesty's rare appearances in London are now listmguisned by a great deal more ceremony than was he case years ago. Upon the occasion of the royal visit of last month, the Queen's carriage was drawn by four superb bays, driven by postilions and preceded by out- riders. The Princess of Wales, on the other hand, has, of late years, dispesvd as far as possible with all pomp and ceremony when travelling or driving. Except upon ,tate or semi-state occasions. Her Royal Highness has great objection to more than two horses being used in tier carriage, and whenever it is possible to manage without thrm,neither postilions or outriders appear. The Prince of Wales invariably travels with a good deal of pomp and state. The sergeant footman, an im- portant personage in a scarlet coat and waistcoat, with the Prince of Wales's feathers on the gold buttons, and blue plush breeches, has charge of His Royal Highness's luggage, and the Prince's bead valet—wearing plain nark clothes—always travels with him, having at least t wo underlings. The Princess of Wales has arra ng, d to be present on the opening day of the Ladies' Kennel Club Shew,which will be held in the Royal Botanic G irdens on the 23rd and 24th inst, and is exhibiting sine of her beautiful Borzois. Amongst foreign dog., Elm els seems to bel attraritiay, moat at aantion biat now. At the Moscow Dog Show some interesting wolf bunts were plantud for th-se is. hiel, they were splendidly successful. Its dam is the only Berzeis in the world, which holds the eeld medal of the Russian Kennel Clula,and its grandsire obtained a gold medal at MnFICOW for wolf coursing. Her R^yal Highness is very proud of the medals her dogs iee won, ana hae them mounted in Danish red velvet, and displayed in glass eases. A new club is that of the York- shire Terriers, at the head of which is the Countess of Warwick. Dg fancying promises to be more modish than ever this year. _ The "mof quito" net must now be reckoned upnn the modes of the past./sro smart woman wnuld dream of en- veloping her head in one nowadats.The new veil is very shoe, just coming to the tip 64 the nose, and barely covering the top of the ear. Slowly, but surely, tight-lacing has " gone nut," and the girl er woman who sallies forth, exhibiting a waist of wasp-like proportion'', is considered " not good form." Many people seem to think that tight lacing is a comnaratively modern institution, but such is not the csse.Juno as by no means ahoy adding to lair charms by artificially decreasing the size of her waits,. l'H E _EXAMINER FPIDA Y JUNE 10 1898

CAP LT A.L "

Hitherto Russia, so far as is known to British dip:omacy, at least to the British public, has laid outside the sphere of Transvaal intrigue, but is ' now included among toe Powers to which Dr. Leyds is accredited. At the first glance, that does not seem a matter of much moment, as Russia has no interest in the Transvaal,either as a protector of certain forms of religion or financially. Moreover, when the dissensions caused by the raid were at their height, Russia kept aloof, and manifested no desire to mix herself up with them, to the disad- vantage of this country; and, thirdly, Russia's long arm cannot directly stretch into the heart of South Africa. The bearing of a good understanding be- tween the Transvaal and Russia lies in this—that, in the event of our having a quarrel with Russia. the latter Power can always arrange with the Transvaal to make a demonstration of a threatening kind in our rear. The same remark applies to a quarrel with France; and though, in the latter case ' especially, the war would be a purely naval one, it would not be a cheering thought to know that we had to fight the Transvaal as well as one of the European Powers. It is in some such direction as tliat that Dr.Leyds' new mission is most suggestive of evil to us.

It is a very small experiment only which is being made by the military authorities in the way of in- troducing the cycle officially and formally into the army. But the thin end of the wedge has been in- serted, and that of itself counts as a gain. The cyclist is, therefore now a recognised portion of the British army. One permanent corps of 600 men has been added to the establishm,-nt. It will be divided into five companies of 120 privates each, and each company will have a captain, four aubal- terns, one colour-sergeant, four sergeants, four cor- porals, and one bugler. The bicycles have not only been ordered, but are very nearly made for delivery and the men will be taught not only to ride, but to repair their machines. As men are not being enlis- ted for the purpose, but are being drawn for exist- ing regiments, and probably have never sat upon a machine in their lives, they will have to be trained for the purpose.

There is only too much reason to fear that a parliament of railway shareholders will speedily forget and forgive the action of the railway corn- ' panies in refusing the usual Saturday afternoon facilities to volunteers going to and returning from practice in summer. But it will not be forgotten by the public at large, especially by that growing band of agitators who are now doing their utmost to push the nationalisation of the railways to the front. We must at least wait till a Liberal Govern- ment is in power, and a strong man is appointed Chairman of Committees, before this latest phase of monopoly bears due fruit. It should at least ring- guest to the Committees on Railway Bills the ex- pediency of examining their provisions with a jeal- ous eye. In the meantime, the common and feder- ated action of several of the great lines has broken down, partly under the representations of the War Office, and partly under the dread of worse befall- ing them on the eve of the autumn manceuvres. One line after another has backed out, and the directors' union has in this matter collapsed ; but there is not another country in Europe where a similar thing could have happened.

I hear that there is a good deal of dissatisfaction among some of the Scotch members in regard to treatment of Piper Findlater by the War office. It is contended that as he is no longer on active ser- vice, and is not capable of being so again in conse- luence of his injuries, ha ought not to be debarred from getting his living by appearing in public if it pleases him to do so.

Yorkshire has beaten Lancashire in the first of the semi-finals ot the Parliatnentary Golf Tourna- ment. This, it must be said was not expected. Mr. Fison had to give Mr. Kemp three strokes in the round, and there was a general belief that these odds would he too heavy for him. He played,how- ever, a very steady game, while Mr. Kemp failed to keep up the remarkable form he showed in some of his earlier matches ; and so the member of the Don- caster division won by two and one to play. Mr. Wanklyn and Mr. Sei.on-Katr will not play till next week, and there will probably be considerable delay in the final match, for Mr inson is under an en- gagement to leave London for some i weeks u the course of the next two or three days.

-- I hear that some very influential men on both sides ot polities have joined the provisional com- mittee for organising toe national memorial to Mr. Gladstone. Ot this provisional committee the Duke of Westminster bas consented to become the Chal man,and he will no doubt be confirmed in that posi- tion when the permanent committee is nominated. Mr. Balfour has also consented to act, but I rather imagine that this movement, though national in character, has its lons et origo among Liberals. It will receive support from all quarters,and I think a special effort will be made to give workingmen's clubs throughout the country opportunities ot tes- tifying to the deceased statesman's character and merits. The difficulty will probably be tO deal Ike- fully with the very large fund that will be raised. This scheme does not conflict with the Liberal me- morial which is to be launched.

The German Ministry of Commerce is considering a suggestion made by somal of the mercantile houses engaged in the Eastern trade for the estab- lishment at Kiao-Chow of a permanent Government Commercial Department to assist and as far as pos- sible supervise the export trade to China. The idea is not viewed with favour by the batiks, who through their agencies in China conduct a good deal of business for manufacturing and exporting firma.

The recent comparative return of the navies of the Great Powers is proving a most unfortunate document for the Government, for it has set all the experts analysing it, with results not creditable to ourselves. The most overwhelming and telling anal, sis which I have yet seen has just appeared in one of our evening papers, in which the condi- tion of the rival fleets is shown in 1889 and in the present year. As the return published the other day is one of a series of five which have been moved for at intervals since 1886. the figures are authentic so far as they go. In 1880 France and Russia had 52 armoured ships complete and 22 building, making a total of 75, some of which, however, were classed as ineffective. At the same date we had 62, all which were effective, and we were about to build 15, making a total ot 77. At present France and Russia have 87 armoured ships completed and 30 building, making 117, while we have 80 completed and cnINT 20 building, making 10b. In 1889 the B. British fleet had a superiority of 3 per cent., now ' In an inferiority of 10 per cent. This refers I merely to battleships and BOMB classes of cruisers, i but the return shows still more alarming results n other departments of our naval force.

Nothing has been heard for a year past of the committee which was appointed,or which appointed itself, to foster an Anglo-French entente. It does not appear to have achieved any result,for the feel- ing of the Colonial party has not been modified to- wards this country. The tone of the French Press has not improved, the sentiment of the Parisian public has not been made more friendly, and the French Government has pursued its aggressions and clings to them with inflexible persistence. It may be said that the cause of this failure lies in the fact that the committee was not a very influential one; but it lies probably much more in the constant Cry of some politicians,brith Ministerial and Opposi- tion, that resistance to French demands, however, unjust, meant war, and that the subject in dispute was not worth the loss which war would entail. It is to be hoped that the corresponding committee, which is in process of formation for fostering a good understanding with the United States, will not prove equally barren. It starts its work under the better auspices of being an extremely strong com- mittee, so far as yet constituted. The Church, the aristocracy, influential laity, representative men from all bodies of Dissenters, from literature, law, science, and art, and even ladies, are all represented Upon it, Such a broad expression of British feeling ought to find a warm response on the other side of the Atlantic.

CHILDREN OF CUBA: A 'TALE OF LOVE AND WAR

Br GRAHAM REDPATH, Author of "The Balled Press. Gang," "When

Things Went Wry," &c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS :—Dr. Jacca Cardonna, who has lost money in speculation, is leaving Spain with his wife and two daughters, Castilia and Coria, to make his fortune in Cuba. The girls both leave sweethearts behind them—Leon and Castello. Dr. Cardonna makes his fortune in Cuba and thinks of returning home with his family. Meanwhile, during a holiday in England, Leon and Castello have made friends with Vigo, another Spanish visitor. At Havana a mysterious man named Vargas becomes the close friend of the doctor, and helps him to obtain a fortune.

CHAPTER VII. AS HONEST AS VARGAS.

The doctor had speculated successfully, and having retrieved his fortune he naturally took a rosy view of things, his sole aim being to replace his family in its former proud position. The physician sold his property, and placed the bulk of his money, two hundred thousand dollars, in the hands of his trusty friend Vargas, the prin- cipal banker in Havana, for safe keeping till his return.

Coria and Castello met in Spain, though the latter fought very ahy of Leon. Castello had failed to correspond with his cousin, and he feared Leon, knowing he would be aware of the cause. Some hot words were exchanged by the cousins, but their anger passed off. Coria, however, flatly told him what her suspicions were. But Castello denied that he led a dissolute ife. Suspicion fell on Leon, for Castello remembered some of his words in London, which he imagined at the time contained a threat. Now he was sure of it.

He kept away, in order to repay his sweetheart for her temper, and make Leon conscience-smitten for his share in the squabble. At last he came across Leon and his two cousins in the public gardens at Toledo, and after the concert accom- panied Coria home, the lovers becoming reconciled to each other on the way.

Castilia told Leon something more concerning the man whose face so strongly impressed him. Vargas had proposed to her! Even had she been free she would have spurned him and treated his proposals with the utmost contempt, and she told hitn so, despite his assertion that had she not been engaged her father would have favoured his. suit.

Vargas had swallowed his chagrin with a wry face, begging forgiveness and her secresy. Thus the doctor knew nothing of the matter.

Vargas had longed to become allied with a family of old Castilian blood, and here he was flouted by one of their proud daughters, whose father he could buy over, gold for gold, a dozen times at least. There was that in the man which could not prevent him taking some revenge on those who crossed him. Besides, he had been badly smitten by the charms of Castilia. Before the slave trade was so much kept in check Cuba was the transatlantic depot for large cargoes of African slaves, and their profitable dis- posal either to Cuban planters or those from the Southern States. Rapid fortunes were made, the profits of a single voyage sometimes sufficing to make these traffickers in humanity rich, had they not gambled so freely in Havana, which had become the Mecca for the penniless pilgrims to the shrine of Fortune, men despairing and desperate, and not too scrupulous as to how they propitiated their god of gold. What Sacramento and San Francisco were, and what Monte Carlo is to-day, Havana was at that time.

Vargas arrived as a poor adventurer with a poorly-lined purse. But he was sharp-witted. Seeing how crowded already was the way to fortune, he left the beaten track, and posed as a religious devotee.

In two years he saved sufficient to commence a small brokerage business and a home for the accommodation of sailors. By the reputation Vargas gained through his honest dealing with sea- faring men he soon rose to a better position. He took the savings of hundreds of sailors every year, and never failed to repay the sums he received from them. He WAS the best known man on the quay, the friend alike of trader, planter, and skipper, so that little by little all kinds of business were thrown in his way, and soon he had the most varied and most flourishing establishment in the town.

And he liked the place of his adoption, or he would have left it with a fortune at his back. He preferred to remain, and became the chief banker in the West Indies, possessing branches at several of the larger islands in the group. "As honest as Vargas" became a familiar phrase in Cuba. In the city his new position gave him the entrea to some of the best houses, and especially that society which he wished most to reach, the old Castilian families. Thus it came about that he was introduced to and received with open arms in the doctor's household.

Castello scarcely got out of one scrape when he plunged headlong into another. It was a further step in his downward career. Only a month elapsed before the influence of his cousin Coria had vanished, and with it all restraint and respect. In one of those miserable resorts devoted to midnight orgies he one night brutally assaulted a woman. She had charmed but to tantalise, and a heavy blow stretched her senseless on the floor.

To the officer who demanded his name he gave another, remembering he was quite unknown in Madrid, and Leon's name would serve him better. He escaped lightly enough, for the woman would not press the charge, but some strong comments on his cowardice were made. Yet he turned the incident to a vile and low purpose. Castello actually wrote a letter to Castilia in which he stated that Leon was a mere impostor, of the most polished sort. He felt bound as a relative to make known the true facts of his latest escapade, enclosing a periodical of the semi-satirical type, describing Leon as a man believed to be the last lineal descendant of an old Toledo family, &c., and, though not giving his rank and recent honours, mentioning that he held a commission in the Spanish Legion. He had hoped, said Castello, that Leon would have really behaved better, especially after his mother's death, and that was why he had delayed mention of the matter, and also because Leon had made him swear not to betray him. Whatever unpleasantness this might cause he could not help, strongly though he regretted it, doing what he divined to be right. It was as grave an effusior., as it was grossly untrue, and the whole libel was so seriously and so plausibly worded that it was calculated to succeed; the printed account confirm- ing the whole tissue of falsehood. In due time the letter reached Castilia. At first she would not believe the statement. Then she read it again, and the cold confirmatory printed account twice over.

She destroyed the letter and the miserable paper, for she accepted the proofs. She was hot and angry to think that she had come in contact with such a man, so smooth of face and speech, so foul at heart. He, the self-elected critic of his fellow men Pshaw But her idol was utterly east down and shattered, and her spirit broken. This was her reward for constant tenderness and truthfulness. How his sophistries had blinded her eyes to his deceit!

She returned Leon's portrait and ring, cancelled the engagement in a curt note, and said she need not trouble him with the painful reasons for her action.

Day by day she pined and fretted for a reply, but when it reached her she was ill and feverish, and stealing frpm her bed in the absence of her mother she placed it on the fire, unopened and unread. And when too late she regretted what she had done in her state of semi-delirium.

===== 0002601_18980610_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER VIII. THE HAVANA NANKIN.

Trouble sometimes fills the air, and certain seasons seem suited for revenge. No sooner had Castilia recovered than the Cardonna family sus- tained a shock which drove them all to the verge of wild despair. After discharging his hotel bills, and paying minor expenses in connection with the purchase of the new home, Dr. Cardonna found he had little money left. Of course there was the large sum be had left in charge of his banker, and lie must &Om a little on that capital till the divi- dends of tin few interests he had retained fell due.

On the day ot tneir arrival the doctor called upon his old friend Vargas. They drank two bottles of wine whilst chatting about the chief incidents of the trip, and of Vargas's rapid and great success in the meanwhile.

It had been a busy, brisk time in the island during the doctor's absence, quite a speculative boom, and Vargas had made hay whilst the sun shone. There was no difference in his habits, nor between the relationship of the two friends, save that Vargas was a little dilettante in his tastes, and dressed with greater care. But the doctor was honestly glad to hear of his friend's success, and congratulated him on his wisdom as a financier, the growth of his worldly possessions, and rallied him on the score of marriage. So several nights of good fellowship pss4td, until the doctor needed his money, which, by the way, had not been men- tioned since his return, for he did not desire to a ppear anxious.

Ire had hoped Vargas would mention the subject o if-handedly, by saying: "Well, your money is all right; shall you be wanting any shortly, or are you going to leave the field of speculation to me, and allow me a fair chance?" But he did not do 80.

The doctor, intending to get a small portion of the large sum he had left with his friend, called upon Vargas one morning.

The banker was delighted to see him, and was quite demonstrative. He insisted on the doctor having wine.

At length the physician intimated his business, with that difficulty which friends sometimes ex- perience in speaking of such matters.

--------• Vargas stared vacantly at the doctor, as though he did not understand him, and sharply asked: "What money ? "

Dr. Cardonna, with a quiet firmness that be- tokened his more stable qualities, replied that he wished to withdraw a portion of the three hundred thousand dollars which he had entrusted to the banker's keeping. So unperturbed did he seem that Vargas might have readily imagined that he had not heard the query. Vargas nerved himself up for the effort. He stared with well feigned astonishment, and answered: "Why, how extraordinary! I don't really understand you. You never placed any money with me!" Surprise and pain were pictured in the doctor's face. His lips twitched nervously, but he tried to control his feelings. Vargas could not be joking, his demeanour shelved this, but the doctor scarce knew what he said.

"Come, be serious," he appealed, with a choking sensation in his throat, and a dull leaden pain at his heart. "It is business we are talking now, strict business, and I need my money. "But lam serious," Vargas protested. It is you, not I, who jest. I cannot comprehend your meaning. I am at an utter loss to apprehend what you want. Money to me !—you never gave me any money to keep." "No, not to keep, only to guard and return," said the doctor sharply in reply. The air seemed to thicken, and neither the banker nor the doctor was able to breathe quite freely. Both were excited, though they subdued their feelings wonderfully. But the blood rushed to their heads, and the pulsing of their hearts seemed almost audible.

Naturally, the doctor was the more perturbed of the two, as the moments on which all his hopes depended sped by, and he betrayed his symptoms of anxiety by trembling from head to foot. The flashing eyes of his companion quivered in satisfaction at the sight.

With much pathos he recounted the uninterest- ing details of the transaction, in the hope of brushing up his friend's memory. It had been a mere matter of handing over so much paper.

The appeal was lost on the man, whose heart was set upon a dark and deep revenge. A flat denial was all that Vargas vouchsafed. His friend was either mad or drunk.

The treachery was app'arent, and the doctor fiercely exclaimed:

"You got my money, and, by heaven, you shall repay! Mad I may well be, but beware of a mad- man's revenge!" Vargas shuddered at the threat, but otherwise he was perfectly calm, now that the crisis had come, almost calmer than he expected he should be. "I challenge you to shew your proofs. You are entirely mistaken!"

As the startled doctor stared in the cold eyes and rigid countenance, he realised in a moment that he had absolutely no proof of the transaction. He essayed to denounce the betrayer, but the effort of speech was beyond him. The bulk of his fortune had been entrusted to a bosom friend without a scrap of paper as security or proof. Vargas had encompassed him with wiles and deceit to his utter ruin. With one bitter glance at the swindler, who, vibrating with fear and trembling, laid his hand on a stiletto, the old doctor quitted the room. He rushed out of the banker's home, scarce knowing, and caring less, where he was, where he went, or what he did.

"I'm almost penniless!" ejaculated the heart- broken man when he entered the apartments at the hotel where his family were staying, and sank exhausted into a chair.

His explanation and self-recriminations were distressing. It was a sorry scene. Had death claimed the head of the household their grief could scarcely have been more poignant. The doctor spoke bitterly of Vargas, and became silent. His head was downcast like his heart, and his eyes were fixed vacantly on the floor.

• All four were engaged in gloomy thoughts on the change next day would bring. Coria and her mother wept; Castilia and her father thought bitterly instead; and so sat these four till well into the night. That was how they received the great blow.

"Leon was right, said the doctor at last.

Castilia wept, for in her heart she loved Leon as fondly as ever, and that was the first time she had heard his name for some time.

"I wonder if he has ever recalled where he first met Vargas? "

"If he ever did meet him," said the broken- hearted mother, who grieved for her daughters more than herself.

"Leon musts have seen him," said Coria ; "he Was so positive." "But what motive has Vargas had for this dastardly robbery ? " said the mother.. "That I cannot divine; it completely banks me," replied the doctor. "I think I—ean—enlighten you," said Castilla, between her sobs. "You can?" said the mother in surprise. "Yes," said Castilia meekly. "I rejectedlhim, and this is his cowardly revenge." "He wished to marry you, my love 1" said the doctor indignantly. "Yes," replied his daughter; "and it is I who am to blame for our ruin."

"Better be beggared than be mated to such a scoundrel! How dare he ask for your hand!"

"The villain!" said the mother. "If I had only known!" said the doctor, whose cup of misery had now been filled. "I should have told you, father, but he threatened me. He swore if I failed to keep the subject secret he would seek his revenge on those I loved."

"And no one knew?" asked the doctor. "No one, save Leon."

"Had I only knolim, I lihould have detested him from that moment, and we should have been far out of the reach of his vengeance. But these are vain regrets. I cannot blame you; you did well to spurn his offer."

Grief-stricken Cast ilia kissed her father ana mother ere she sought the solitude of. her own room. She prayed for her poor parents astt pleaded for Divine comfort for them in their great _ _ _

•Then she lay down to sleep, and dreamt of the happy days when Leon and she had no thoughts of sorrow or separatitn.

i.' It was hard to bear, but the Cardonnas bore their ichanged fortune patiently and with dignity. They proceeded to a poor but quiet quarter of the city, determined to begin their obscure life bravely and cheerfully. And they carried all their loveliness and tenderness of character into that half-deserted street; and the nobility of their disposition stood out in relief in its humble setting of bare walls and plain furniture.

Dr. Cardonna made no secret of the swindle, and openly denounced Vargas. Had he not been driven nearly to madness he might have been less frank; but the rumour could not be overtaken or the truth recalled. It was soon the chief topic of conversation everywhere, but not openly. Commercial men looked upon the doctor as an unsuccessful, soured and spiteful speculator. All

others Toted him a lunatic. 1s story was in. credible, and nothing more so than his assertion that he allowed all this money to go out of his keeping without witness or document of any kind. unhappily, too, for the victim, his destroyer's career in Havana was beyond reproach. Vargso said little or nothing in the affair. He posed so the injured friend, whose confidence had been violated. When material lacks, it is the privilee and prerogative of inventiveness to supply it. so !.t was surmised that the doctor had gambled away, hi, money in Spain, and failing to obtain a large loan from the banker, lie turned round in his chagrin and vilified him senselessly and wickedly. Truth is sometimes not easy to believe or discover. Finding that his word when pitted against the name of Honest Vargas counted for less than the fly on the wheel, the doctor was more exasperated than before. But in the bosom of. his family he found solace, sympathy and strong devotion, which to some extent revived his spirit and his pride. Dr. Cardonna wrote to Castello and asked from him a loan to start him in a humble way as a medical practitioner; for he was bound to do something, and naturally this was the first scheme he and his family had thought of. Besides, it was the least difficult for him.

Castello replied at his leisure, saying he could not comply with the request, and trusting hia engagement with his cousin Coria would be treated as at an end. The union was impossible in the changed circumstances. This simply prostrated the family with a new grief. Their last hope had proved a broken reed.

(7o be continued.)

A SIMPLE SIMILITUDE.

There is a little old red grist mill that stands within half a mile of where I used to live down in the country. I call it red ,meaning that the wooden part of it was red once,and still shows patches of that colour where time and weather have spared them. More than half naill, considering it perpendicularly, is of stone, for It WAS made in those times, and haply in one of the spots, when England could still afford stone as a building L tonal. Oh, I suppose that old mill has stood there for Sixty-live or seventy years. I remember vre, spoke of it as quite a historical structure when I was a boy, and that's longer ago than r I like to think of sometimes. If I make no mistake there are two " run " of stone in it ; quite out of comparison with the great steam grist mills which I- hear are to he found in America, that grind out hue- dreds of barrels of flour in a day. But it served its pnr- pose all the same, and what more can anything do ? The farmers of the country round about, for seasons and seasons on end, brought their wheat, rye, and barley to the old red mill and carried away their grist in the- regular order of things, and so did their song after them, and their sons after them. On rare occasions,whers the water was very low, the mill would have to wait for a rain ; and once in a while something would have to be done to the machinery. But commonly, taking it hi and large, it was as much to be depended on as aril other human contrivance.

Now I have written all this about the mill for the sake of using i° to make plain to my gentle readers an im- portant fact. If, for instance, all the flour, of evenf sort and kind, that has been ground in that mill for tbe past seventy years were in sacks at this blessed moment and all piled up tocether, how much tin you think there would he of it p Would the heap of flour be bigger than the mill itself? Would it be several times bis,,ger ? If you have a head for figures you can cipher out some reasonable answer to this question while I copy a letter that a woman writes about her son. Afterwards we will see what the nN mill and the young man have to dO with each other.

"For many years," she says, "my son Alexastder suffered from a bad stomach. He had no relish fer food, and after eatinz had pains around the chest and rides. Fie suffered a deal from asthma and had die' culty in breathing. He was often so bad that be bad to -fiaht for his breath. He got very weak.and for week. he was confined to his bed. For feur years he was • puir weakly laddie, and was barely able to get about. He took medicines and bad applications to the °best, but got no better. In January, 1894, he vad in a sinsli hook left at tha 110,3 PC about Mother epugel's Cantle Syrup, and got a bottle of this medicine. After taki•O it he felt that it was doing him good. His breathier was easier, and all food agreed with him. He continued with it, and after a time was strong and hearty. fre has since kept in good health and can do any kind or work. Yeu can publish this statement as you think (Signed) (Mrs.) Ann Smith, East Bellehigblasb, Ballifl dallocb,Elgin, N.B.,July Stb, 1897." The relation between the mill and the young man probably now clear to you. Still we will trace out the NW nese so as to have it on record for future reference. Not only Alexander Smith, but everybody else, has witbi/e him an organ which may be likened to a mill—namellr ' the stomach. As the mill turns grain into four go the stomach turns flour and other food into the sum avel substance of the human body—the latter by far the more complicated and wonderful operation. When the Tnill runs slowly for lack of power, or the stones are well' smooth from neglect, the mill grinds less dour and of • coarser quality. And when the stomach gets out of order,, aa that of Alexander Smith for some reason did, it doell poorer work and less of it. This is what we call' dyspepsia—a word which in plain English meanS• difficult digestion. Now if the water should run out of

===== 0002601_18980610_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
pond faster than it runs in, the surface would becoin

low, and the sodden, unsightly shores the breeding grounds of malaria and other unwholesome influences to people living near by. Bad epidemics of fevers bal. often been started that way. Similarly when the stomach I, unable to supply digested nutriment the latter be comes first weak and then diseased all over, because ie.' stead of being ground or prepared by the stomach mill the food stops inside and rots.

This was the ailment of which Mother Seigel's Syrur cured Alexander Smith. It prepared his stomach-mill. Thus, haying made our point, we end (Nu parable.

THE ST. NEOT'S POISONING CASE.

SENTANCE OF DEATH ON HORSFORD. The trial of Walter Horsford for the murder of hilf cousin Annie Holmes at St. Neot's was concluded 00 Monday at Huntingdon Assizes. Mr. Justice Hawkio6 In butruning Up said that if anyone administered poise) or other noxious thing to a woman in a case or this Irina and she died In consequence the person who ad" ministered the poison was guilty of murder. The jury' had been invited to take a very unfavourable view a Mrs. Holmes, but there was no evidence that she WO the profligate she was represented to be. Even if she were, however, that would not aid the prisoner. flig Lordship went on to eau:nine in detail the point raised for the defence, observing in regard to most of them that they were unsupported by evidence. 01 commented also upon the fact that the prisoner ha' called no evidence to show what he did with all the strychnine he bought, or where he was on the day %viler; it was et ggested be visited Mrs. Holmes and induce,' her to take the poision. The jury, after half an hour,a absence from court,found a verdict of guilty of murder. Mr. Justice Hawkins in passing sentenc, of death said he thoroughly agreed witt the view the jury had taken. In his opinion the prisoner had been guilty of a bates cruel, treacherous minder. He had put the woman t° death by the most cruel torture which man could invent. His own time on earth would be short.

The DEPUTY MAYO 6 said a great many complaints had been made about there being no pig market in Warring- ton. One should be opened, he contended on the newly paved ground. (Bear, hear). The minutes were adopted. RIVERS COMMITTEE.—THE OLD RIVER BED.

COUNCILLOR BENNETT MOVES :AN AMENDMENT.

The Rivers Committee recommended the Couch i te agree to a proposal of the engineer of the Ship Canal Company to fill up the old river course at Arpley on condition that the Corporation relieved the company from the obligation to construct the culvert and pen- stock in the dam at Arpley. Alderman ROBERTS moved the confirmation of the minutes. Be explained that the negotiations between the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company some time ago led to an arrangement being arrived at under which the company had to do certain things, amongst others being the conetruction of a penstock at Arplev, the object of which was to allow of the flushing of the old river bed by the action of the tides rooming up the Ship Canal. At the same time that the penstock was agreed to be constructed the company obtained powers to close the tidal openings in the canal. The result of the closing of those openings had been that the tides of the Ship Canal and the river now reached Warrington almost simultaneously. The Borough Surveyor had made special observations on the subject and his report went to show that the penstock, if constructed, would be inoperative for the purposes for which it was originally intended. Be wanted the Council to appre- ciate the fact that so far as they were advised the pen- stock would be inoperative. The condition of the old river bed had been complained of for a long period. Whether it was a danger to health or not was not of it- self absolutely necessary as a condition for the removal of the complaint. If the thing was a nuisance and un- pleasant, and if it could be mitigated and removed, it was their duty as a Corporation to see that it was done. The Manchester Ship Canal Company was responsible for the condition of things which obtained in the old river hed,and it was their duty to remove the obnoxious conditions. The question arose how were they to do it? It had been suggested that the company should fill up that pertion of the river which was left comparatively dry by the receding tides,and that was perhaps the best course to adopt. The objection was that this old river bed might be made into a suitable dock for Warrington. No doubt it might. It was all a question of money, and whether the Corporation of War- rington was prepared to spend the money for making this dockage accommodation. Seeing that the Corporation was not in a position to go into this dock question, and as there was no inclination on the part of the traders to do so, he die not think it was fair to the residents in the neighbourhood to continue the present condition of things a moment longer than necessary because of the possibility at some remote date of turning this niece of the old river bed into dockage for Warrington. Neither did he feel that the filling up of this river bed would act as a perpetual bar to the con- struction of the docks when the public spirit of the town was equal to the occasion. The question arose as to how far the Corporation was justified in giving up any- thing in enabling the Canal Company to carry out a responsibility for which they were liable. If the Cor- poration were giving up anything that was valuable there would be something in that argument. He did not like to undertake the position of making the Ship Canal Company bleed at every possible pore. (Hear, hear.) If there was a chance of getting anything in lieu of this penstock that the Company could afford to give and which would be a source of benefit to Warrington he should perhaps he as keen as anyone else in insisting on the exchange. The only alternative suggestion made— and it was one which had not been disused by the Committee because the communication had only just been reeeived—was that the Corporation should ask the Canal Company to admit the traders of Warrington to enter or leave the Ship Canal at any point between War- rington and Eastham. That matter had been raised be- fore and seriously discussed. 111 confessed that when the agreement was made between the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company the Rivers Committee was under the impression that Warrington traders could en- ter and leave the Canal at any point between Warrington and E ,sthatn, but it was found that the agreement did not cover any other exit except Warrington and East- barn. _ . •

The Mayon You are speaking of the origina agreement Alderman ROBERTS : lam speaking of the last agree_ merit we made with the Ship Canal Company. Con tinning, he said the committee approached the Ship- Canal Company on this point and he was quite satisfied from the case the company put before them that it wasn't any indisposition on the Part of the company that compelled them to refuse their application. Mem- bers of the Council would remember that as soon as the Ship Canal was constructed the whole of the river Weaver traffic bad to pass through the Canal exits at Runcorn and Weston locks. The company told the com- mittee that that traffic was quite as much as they could cope with at those points. Alderman FAIRCLOTIGIT : That is all bosh. Alderman ROBERTS : It may be. That is their case and I confess whi'e giving Alderman Fairclough every credit for believing what he does, I don't altogether Quite agree with him. I don't believe it is all bosh. " ' Councillor BeserneT (continuing) proceeded to explain the result of his visit to the locality, and contended that the smell from the river diversion was greater than that given off by the old river deurse. The Company would use the latter as a tip and would fill it up with mud, slush, and other material dredged from the river. The MAYOR: The Ship Canal Company have full power to fill up the old river bend without asking us.' Councillor Berman thought the company should be brought to realise that that was not the best way of dealing with the matter. He was of opinion that it would be reckless telly for the Corporation to prevent the provision of better facilities for shipping in Warring- ton. The Strip Canal had been open for four years and f not one single step had been taken to utilise it for the benefit of the town. The people in Warrington had never ceased to regret that the Town Council did not force the compaey to make docks. Nobody seemed to take much interest in the matter, and it was more by good luck than good management that the Corporation had got power to construct docks within a period of ten years from the making of the recent agreement. A lay- by had been constructed at Warrington provided with a transporter,and despite the fact that it had been finished six weeks ago not a single ton of cargo had been landed there. It seemed to be part of the policy of the Corpor- ation to let things elide. The scheme he proposed ef making a dock of the old river ceurse was one which commended itself to the approval of the Rivers Com- mittee some years ago. The proposed docks by the Ship Canal Company were a mile away, and it would be very inconvenient for Warrington traders to get to them. He did not wish to badger the Shir Canal Company. With- ftthheeisnhteiprecstasnoafl out wishing to make personal allusion to anyone he was of opinion that Ship Canal matters in Warrington had tb he zi river conducted rather thantoo mtuo much ewithadvantages a eye t which if properly conducted would bring benefits to the whole town. Warrington had natural facilities for be- coming a port. T. the utmost of his power, and to the extent of his ability he should certainly oppose any at- tempt which would prevent the possibility he looked forward to of seeing Warrington Made a port. Councillor TreenON rose to speak, when the Mayo', said: Do you intend. to second the amendment? Councillor Theme : If you will wait a bit I will tell you Mr. Mayor. At the same time he thought it was a matter that deserved considerable discussion, and he thought it would be admittel that there was a considerable nuis- ance existing at Wilderspool-road, and that it was the old river bed that smelled and not the river. He thought that if the Council agreed to the proposal of the Rivers Committee they would be giving up far too cheaply any advantages thee might possess by their agreements with the Ship Canal Cmapany. He was never a great enthu- siast for the Ship Canal, and always held that we had a river at Warrington, which, under proper conditions would have been of much greater benefit to this town than the Ship Canal. He contended that the Ship Canal Company could be compelled to abate the nuisance at Wilderspool, as the t -.vim of Warrington put nothing into the old river bed whi‘lh helped to make the nuisance what ia was to-day,ln that department they had entirely clean hands. His contention was that they had not,and were not likely to get by ac- cepting the recommendation af ths Rivers' Committee, that advantage they should. In his opinion the Ship Canal Company had always got the benefit of Warring ton so far. Mr. Bennett hid said it was adhesive material from the river at Bank Quay. Alderman BOLTON : I'Eu. sand.

Councillor TINNION said he thought if the Council did anything of that sart it would be a great mistake, and the nuisance would he ten times worse than to-day. He hoped the Rivers' Committee would Withdraw this portion of their scheme and go on with something that would be a better bargain with the Ship Canal Company. They were giving up all their advantage with regard to t hepenstock, and he thought it would be a pity if they did not get something in exchange which would be for the benefit of the people of Warrington. Alderman BOLTON thought there had been a good deal of talk and very few facts. In the first place Councillor Bennett was championing a vile nuisance, whiCh had poisoned people in the district for years. Council- lor Bennett wished it tq be dredged out to 20ft so that the increvie I depth of water would give off more aae in hot weather. What was the use of the penstock if it was stopped up 11 Councillor Bennett was a member of the Rivers' Committee and did not understand the C ml- - work. Another thing was that the dues in this portion of the canal were prohibitive to any trader in Warrington. The toll was 31. 61. a ton and who would unload a boat of stone and pay 31. 61. when they could go through the lock into the Mersey for 3,1 ? Mr. Bennett talked about the lay-by being no use and blamed Warrington for it. The tells were such that no trader in Warrington could use it. For the nuisance in question they had a simple remedy. The river-bed was of no use and should be filled up with the sand dredged from the neighbourhood of Mersey Mills. If filled up it would make a solid foundation for wharves, and warehouses. The dock would not be made just there. He thought the best thing would be to get rid of the nuisance, and he hoped the Council would support the Rivers' Committee.

Alderman Faancomn remarked that although he lived in the neighbourhood of this portion of the river he had never had any experience of a bad smell since the Cor- poration constructed a sewer,and had taken the sewage out. There was no doubt that if the weather got very hot, the river bank would smell. He had asked several practical people and they said it would never be a suit- able place for docks,aa the river bed was almost impos- sible as a good foundation. It they could make things better by filling up the river bed he was prepared to ac- cept the scheme,but the action of the Corporation was of such an excellent character that the people in the neighbourhood had been ioerfeetiv satisfied.and he must say that he thanked Dr. Gorman personally and publicly for his pluck in saying that Warrington should be the first to remove its own nuisance. Councillor PIERPOINT contended that as the Corpora- tion had taken its sewage out of the old river bed it was no longer a nuisance. The only cause for filling it up had been removed, and he did not see any advantage to be gained by doing so. With reference to the interjection of Councilloeffeaton that no one wanted the layby be should like to tell Councillor Heaton that personally be would like to make use of it if he could only get some of the shippina owners to allow their vessels to call for cargoes at Warrington. He daresay Councillor John Roberts would find that it was a considerable saving to him if he could get boats to call at the lay-by. Re (Councillor Pierooint) had done his best to get the Rotterdam and London boats to call. The excuse made by the owners for not doing so was that the boats traversed the canal in the nig'lt-time, and that it would not pay them to call for 20 or 30 tons of cargo. He hoped the time was not far distant when Warrington would be able to make it worth while for shippers to let their boats call at Warrington, (Hear, hear.) Al lerman FAIRCLOUGH quite agreed with the action of the Rivers Committee in having the old river bed filled up. He felt that the Corporation had given a great deal to the Ship Canal Company for which they had not received much in return. Warrington traders bad a

great grievance with the Ship Canal Company in being compelled to go out of the Ship Canal at E wham Locks. If they left the canal at Runcorn they would have to nay a toll of 23 31 a ton. The boats of his firm bad to journey through Eastham Locks in order to get to Wid- nes. Ile was sure the Rivers Committee ought to take steps to enable Warrington traders to get out of the canal at any point between Warrington and Esatham. The Comp my had promised to give them an eight feet draft in the river, but they hadn't done so. Councillor FURNESS sail he was inclined to think that if the matter were adjourned for a short time the-Rivers Committee might be able td get some concession from the Ship Canal Company in the direction indicated. Is far as his knowledge served, it seemed to him that it had been a case of conceding to the Ship Canal Com- pany concessions time after time, and as the CiPrports- lion was not compelled to give the concession men- tioned in the resolution be thought they might be able to get something more for it. Ile did not see why the Rivers Committee should give them this concession when the company were compelled to fill up the river. _

Councillor LONGTON supported the amendment. Be thought they would be very rash men, and would be taking up a very responsible position, if they made up their minds that it was right to fill up the old river bed. The interests of the town had been sacrificed to half-a- dozen traders. _ _

I—wEo—ut;iiros:in.ion said the docks which the Corpora- tion was empowered to construct after the lapse of eight " years from now would not be of much use. Alderman Rosman, in reply, said he was somewhat staggered to hear the remark of Councillor Grace that the Corporation would only have power to construct a dock 12ft. deep. He did not know where Councillor Grace got his information from, but it was absolutely incorrect. With reference to the remark of Councillor Loneton that the town's interests had been sacrificed for the benefit of half-a dozen traders, he wished to remind him that theßivers Committee acted is conjunction with a representative committee of traders in Warrington. I . The pint committee consisted of 20 members represent- ing the Town Council and the principal firms of War- riagton. Therefore whatever decisions were arrived at they were the molt of the deliberations of the whole committee. It was cot correct, as Alderman Fairclough said, that they were continuality giving things away to the Ship Canal Company. Alderman ROBERTS proceeded to state what had been given by the Corporation to the Company and detailed the great advantages which had been given in return, including a modified toll for all traders of to-day and all traders in the future. Dealing with the objections of Councillor Bennett he said unfortunately Councillor Bennett did not carry his enthusiastic notions to a practical issue, and it was because of his impractica- bility that he joined issue with him. The Rivers Com- mittee could not be held responsible for the lay-by not being used. He thought the Rivers Committee was be- ing held responsible for a great many things, and why they should be held responsible for the lay-by he could not understan It was only within a week or two that d. that the lay-by had been equipped for trade. Councillor BENNETT : Six weeks. Alderman ROBERTS (continuing) said the lay-by be- longed to the Shin Canal Company and if the company were not smart enough to put before the traders of War- ringtoa sufficient reasons to induce them to bring their goods there, then it was the fault of the Ship Canal ' Company and not the fault of the Rivers Committee. Councillor BBNNETT : We have never tried them. Alderman ROBERTS (continuing) said the lay-by wee a matter of commerce and business. What had the Rivers Committee got to do with it ? It was outside their jurisdiction. If they could assist the traders in any negotiations which took place they would be only too pleased to do so. It was not for the Rivers Committee to take the initiative. With reference to the construc- tion of docks at Warrington they had ten years from 1896 in which to consider the question of making their own decks. The Rivers Committee could not,at present, see it way to deal with the question of docks. The reason why the proposal to utilise the old river bed as a dock was dropped was in consequence of it being found that the area was outside the limit to which the modi- fied tolls applied.and consequently all merchandise would be liable to the full schedule tolls which were very ex- cessive. In conclusion he stated that the recommenda- tion of the Rivers Committee, courled with the under- standing that application would be made for permission to allow Warrington traders to have egress and ingress at the other locks between Warrington and Eastham, was the best solution of the difficulties which had to be faced. Coancillor Timex proposed the adoption of the , minutes. councillor SDIETHORST ascended, and said that he was , a member of several committees, and should be glad to retire as a member of the Gymnasium Committee in favour of the newly-elected member of the Council, Councillor James Evans. Councillor POSRPOINT : That can corns on at the next meeting. The MAYOR: Yes, I think that will be best. The minutes were then agreed to. THE DEVIATION OF SLUTCHER'S LANE. The minutes of the General Purposes Committee con- tained a resolution with reference to the authorized deviation of Slutcher'e-lane. The Town Clerk, at a meet- ing of the commiitee, reported that he had received the award in the arbitration Proceedings between the Cor- poration and Arthur Wilson. The amount was £4,81.0. The MAYOR moved the confirmation of the minutes and said with regard to the estate they would remember 1 that the London and North-Western Railway Company obtained powers under a recent Act to divert Slutcher'e- lane, which they proposed to carry over the lines of the company by bridges. The width of the authorized new roads and bridges was 22 feet, and the Council some time ago decided to request the company to widen the bridges and the approach to them to 40 feet in width. The company were prepared to do this at the cost of the Corporation. The only item which concerned the Corporation was the one of £2,775. This covered the cost of widening the incline and approach from Wilson Patten-street and the bridges. The road leading from the proposed bridges to the three arches was not of the same importance; and he did not think the Corporation would be justified in incurring the cost of widening that read. They were aware that Mr. John White,on behalf of Lord Winmarleigh's trustees, was prepared to continue the road from the bridges across land belonging to Lord Winmarleigh at his own ccst, and they would see from the estimate, the company had offered to contribute £1340 towards the making of the road. The Town Clerk , informed him that he was in communication with the London and North-Western Railway Company on the ' important question of the maintenance of the roads. The assent of Lord Newton to the proposed new roads across Arpley weuld be required, but from a conversation with Mr.Pardey it was not likely that any objection would be raised. Alderman BOLTON explained that the resolution did not commit the Council to the spending of any money whatever. The Town Clerk had simply to aseertain further details of the proposed work. TEE FEATHER'S HOTEL ARBITRATION. ALDERMAN SUTTON'S ACTION SEVERELY CRITICISED.

night. N 4 ono had a greater regard for Alderman Sutton than he had, but he should like to refer to the recent arbitration proceedings. Councillor Ramis-yr : I consider the worthy Alderman's action is quite indefensible, if not illegal. If not illegal it is most indiscreet. No man can serve two masters, and if Alderman Sutton prefers his professional fees to the public) interest he should retire from his position as a member of the Corporation. I think that is the opinion of 19 out of 20 persons. I congratulate the town upon the satisfactory end of the award, but I am sorry that I have to couple my congratulations .with this criticism.

Alderman'Bovrow : Does Councillor Bennett get any fees, By b,eing auditor ? Alderman SUTTON : Yes. I don't get anything. Alderman Feaercomn referring to the question of the bridge in Slutcher's-lane, said he did not see why such an enormous etructure was required. : Alderman Suwon said the sum of £2,775 appeared to him to be a very large one to pay either for the roads or the bridges at Arpley. He failed to see what benefit there would be to the ratepayers. If any people would benefit it would be the' owners of the property. (Hear. hear.) Why should the Corporation provide now roads and wide bridges? Alluding to his connection with the recent arbitrations, Alderman Sutton said : Councillor Bennett has been pleased to speak about this matter. He has had it on his mind for a long time,and at last ho has been able to speak out what he desired to say. He has made a complaint with respect to my conduct in refer- ence to the recent arbitration. After the explanation which I have already made through the newspapers I do not consider it necessary that I should trouble myself by replyikg to the attack which balsas made upon me to- night. Some people,like Councillor Bennett for instance, are of a sensitive nature,and are often disposed to strain at a gnat when they could very easily swallow a camel. (Laughter.) They can make sentimental objections to almost every matter which may come under the notice of the Council of a very trifling nature, whilst they can support any pet scheme of their own, however expen- sive or extravagant it may be. That is the character of my friend Councillor Bennett,and no doubt his object to some extent is to gain popularity. (" No, no," and laughter.) Alderman FIIANCOMB : He is only a dreamer.(Rene wed laughter.) Alderman Sorrow: I have had a great many attacks made upon me of late,and I cannot help referring to the one which was made upon me by a published circular and advertisement which appeared on the walls during the late election in the Whitecross Ward. Everyone I know pronounces that to be most scurrilous and offen- sive.

Alderman BOLTON: It is not on the minutes. Alderman SUTTON: It has been so considered by many of my friends. Alderman ROBERTS rose to speak when Alderman FRANCOMB (interrupting) said : You are innocent. You have nothing to do with it. Alderman ROBERTI: But I should like some informa- tion about this matter. I slioqld like to know if members are allowed to introduce matters here which have origin- ated outside. . .

Alderman SUTTON (to the Mayor) : Whatever the Town Clerk's opinion may be I shall ask for fair play. The regulations of the Council are in your hands, and as long as I am attacked in the way I have been I claim to have the privilege of a reply. Alderman Ireascoam: Alderman Roberts had nothing to dojwith it. Alderman ROBERTS : I Alderman Sutton in order in referring to matters originating outside ? Alderman SUTTON : We are always to accord with what you say. Alderman ROBERTS: I ask for the Mayor's ruling. The MAYOR : I think Alderman Sutton has a right to refer to the matter, but I hope he will make it as brief as possible. I wish to put an end to these personalities. Alderman ROBERTS : Then I shall reply to him. Alderman Surron : That circular was so offensive that I have been urged by many to take action in the matter. Alderman BOLTON : What is tho cii cular ? I don't know anything about it. Alderman SUTTON : I have finished with it. The cir- cular was a dishonest way of securing votes. What I wish to say, in conclusion, is this, that as long as I am a member of the Council I shall consider it my duty to support every ratepayer in the borough, and if I consi- der he is being unjustly dealt with by any committee of the Corp Ration I shall do my best to prevent that com- mittee carrying out what it proposes to do. Let me say further that at the present moment I feel thankful that I have not been a party to bringing about the rejoicings which many people seem to have made in reference to the result of the arbitration. I shall never gloat over it, and I am thankful that I never attempted to gloat over the fact that a ratepayer of this borough has been forced to sell his property for what is considered to be less than the actual value. . . . . . _

_ Alderman Rosman : It was not my intention to have said anything about this matter, because I have had an opportunity of doing so in the public press, but as the Alderman has taken up a position to-night in regard to what Councillor Bennett has said which is in my opinion absolutely unjustifiable and prejudicial te the beet inter- ests of the town, I feel bound to say a word or two. I take no the initial position, and say that every member of the Council who comes here is charged with a respon- sibility for the general body of the ratepayers. That is the purpose for which he is elected—to do what he can for the best interests of the general body of the rate- payers. What is Alderman Sutton ? He is chairman of the committee which initiated the improve's:lent in Bridge-street, and then he goes into court against the Corporation, and against the ratepayers of Warrington. Alderman BOLTON : We ought not to be judges of this sort of thing. Alderman ROBERTS : I am speaking to facts, and lam going to get the facts out. Alderman SUTTON : Mr. Roberts interrupted me and I am going to imerrapt him. I ask Mr. Roberts to what extent I have benefVed by the action I have taken ; and whether it is not my duty to protect the ratepayers? I was engaged independently of any connection I had with the Corporation in toe first place to value the pro- perty. Then I was asked to support that value before the court. If I bad refused to do so they could have got me there on on a subpcena. I should have been obliged to go under any circumstances, and to accuse me of dis- honest conduct is a very irrproper thing, and I am sur- prised that Alderman Roberts and Councillor Bennett should have b-en so bitter as they have been because I was obliged to take the part I did in my own private business. Whether I liked it or not I was compelled to do no. (Interruption.) The MAYOR I roust beg there may be no further in- terruption. lam very sorry this discussion has taken place. Alderman Surrost : Alderman- Roberts has already said enough. Alderman ROBERTS : lam perfectly satisfied with the position I took up in the press, and was content to leave the matter there, but as Alderman Sutton has referred to the matter in reply to Councillor Bennett, and has made remarks which are not justifiable and are not in the best interests na the good government of the town, I feel bound to justify the position I have taken up. The position is this, Alderman Sutton was professionally en- gaged against the interest of the Corporation. If he will assure me that he took that appointment without fee or reward it will put an entirely new complexion on the matter. Alderman SIITToN : Do you do such things? The MAYOR Do be quiet. Alderman ScrTrox : I will become a manager of a limited liability Company. That's the way to do it. At this stage of the discussion several members of the Council rose and left the table. Alderman BOLTON If we have to vote upon Alder- man Sutton's conduct I will it here, but If net lam going. The MAYOR : You must sit down cr I will retire from the chair. Alderman BOLTON then left the room. Alderman ROBERTS : I think there are sufficient members who have enough self-respect and regard for the interests of Warrington to rennin to keep a quorum and listen to what I have to say. In Alderman Sutton's case the greatest possible importance was attached to his evidence by the counsel for the plaintiff, Mr. Wilso 12 because of a fact that he was a member of the Corpora- tion,and chairman of the Street Improvement Commit- tee which initiated the improvement. If you will read counsel's address you will see that the greatest import- ance was attached to the value of his evidence. Alderman Simon The committee had no voice in the matter. Let us have the truth. Alderman ROBERTS : You are getting the truth and don't like it. What Alderman Sutton went into court to do was to ask the court to give between £3,000 and bi,ooo more money than the umpire had awarded, and nearly double the price the Corporation were advised was the value of the property. Well,if that is consistent with what in these enlightened days is considered to be proper conduct on the part of an alderman of a corpor- ation I have nothing more to say in the matter. Alderman SfrrToN ; My valuation was more in com- parison than what the Corporation offered. The discussion then dropped and the minutes of the committee were confirmed.

WALKING DAY.

The MAYOR moved :—That on the occasion of the Public procession of Sunday scholars, on Friday, theist July, 1898, no cart, carriage, lurry, or any vehicle (except tarries or other vehicles used for conveying chil- dren in the procession and mail carte), and no horse shall be allowed to remain in or travel along any of the fallowing streets between the hours of nine a.m. and 12 30 p.m., vir :—Bevrsey-street, Bold-street, Bridge. street, Huttermarket-street, Hormemarket-street, Warn- street, SAnkey-stroet, Parker-street, and Wilson Patten- street. The above order shall cease to apply to any Ot the above-named streets as 80011 as the Procession, in its proper order, has passed along any such street. Alderman FAANCOMI seconded, and it was agreed to. The Connell then rose.;
