CYCLING AND CYCLISTS.

No. lII.—THE EVOLUTION OF

THE CYCLE.

DT CHARLES P. SISLEY (EDITOR OF THE "CYCLE").

-.-APA„., •

It is just thirty years ago that we in England were surprised by the appearance at some of the London gmnasia, and in a few of the principal thoroughfares, of a mechanical contrivance con- sisting of two wheels in a line, connected by an iron framework, on which a saddle was placed. On this saddle a man sat, propelling the machine by means of treadles on the front wheel, and steering by means of a handle-bar.

The new contrivance came from Paris, and the greatest wonder was expressed at the possibility of being abls to balance oneself on such a machine; it was looked upon with little favour by the pre- judiced public—at the best it was considered an ingenious French toy—and he would have been a bold man who then ventured the prediction that the riding of machines not dissimilar to these would become almost as common as walking, would create a new and enormous industry, and would institute little less than a social revolution in the country. Yet these have been some of the effects of the introduction of those primitive types of cycle into England in the 'sixties, and the object of this brief sketch is to shew how this silent revolution has come about.

In its early days the bicycle was entirely an instrument of pleasure and recreation. Bicycling was a hobby confined to the well-to-do and the enthusiast. The devotee of the wheel had in those days to stand a fire of

INCESSANT CHAFF AND RIDICULE

such as we, who nowadays buy a "bike" as a matter of course, as one would buy a hat or a golf stick, have no conception of. To be a bicyclist was to declare oneself independent of conventionality, and become a target for the jests of one's friends, and in a far greater degree, of course, for the gibes of the übiquitous street boy. Only an enthusiast could possibly remain loyal to the wheel, for the apprenticeship he had to serve was a severe one. Machines were clumsily put together, accidents were numerous, and the discomforts and annoyances too frequent to recount.

But there must have been plenty of compensatory pleasure in propelling those heavy old "bone- shakers "—as these early cycles were dubbed—or the pastilne would not have lasted a single season. Instead of dying, more and more Englishmen took it up; the French manufacturers were kept busy supplying us with new machines; accounts of rides accomplished on them began to appear in our newspapers, and gradually, as with the motor-car to-day, it began to dawn upon the English people that there was something in the idea after all. When the awakening did come English makers began to spring up, and the building of velocipedes was commenced in earnest. Then the French machines were needed no longer. Although a great many bicycles were constructed in London, the quaint old city of Coventry gradually came to be considered the headquarters of the new industry. Ribbon-making was in a bad way, watch- making was depressed, and some of the Coventry firms started to manufacture sewing-machines, and, as a sort of side-line, bicycles.

The bicycle of those days had equal-sized wheels, and was driven direct from the axle of the front wheel. Strangely enough, the bicycle of to-day has many of the characteristics of those earliest mounts. Its wheels are equal-sized, the rider sits in much the same position relatively and steers in much the same way, the main difference being that instead of driving direct on to the front wheel we now drive the rear wheel, by means of a chain or by connecting rods and bevel cogs. But in the 'seven- ties inventors had not advanced to this stage, and it was not until the bicycle had gone through a multitude of changing shapes and sizes and styles that the original shape was in the main reverted to. The first modifications which took place were in THE SIZE OF THE WHEELS.

The front one was gradually made larger, and the rear wheel became smaller by degrees and beauti- fully less. The result was to produce a most sym- metrical and elegant machine, and it became the proud aim of the enthusiast to ride the highest possible wheel. The craze—like all crazes—soon killed itself. Riding became too dangerous for the ordinary man, and the thoughts of inventors were turned in the direction of "safety" bicycles.

In the early 'eighties bicycles in all manner of shapes and sizes were designed for making tiding safer, but very few of the ideas were really practi- cable. Perhaps the most popular at this time were the Extra-Ordinary, a bicycle which enabled its rider to sit far behind the centre of the front wheel, and the Facile, a more lowly mount, worked by levers. The "Kangarocl" was a diminutive type of bicycle, front-driven by means of chains, and it had a con- siderable vogue. But nothing really met the case until the earliest fashion of two equal-sized, or almost equal-sized, wheels was reverted to. This was done in the Rover Safety bicycle, and in this machine the driving was transferred to the rear wheel by means of a chain. The rider sat virtually between the wheels, and steered the front one by means of forks which raked backwards considerably. It was not long before practical riders saw in the Rover type of bicycle the want that had so long been felt. Gradually it was taken up. Big feats began to be accomplished on it; its manufacture in modified forms by nearly all the manufacturers was commenced, and a new era dawned for the cycle industry. To mount and ride a bicycle was no longer a semi-acrobatic performance. Elderly men, aye, and our wives and daughters were able to share the delights of bicycling with the young and athletic members of the community, who had hitherto monopolised the pastime, and the result of the adoption of this new and more rational type of wheel we see in the present popularity of cycling all over the world. It must not be supposed, however, that the new type was adopted at a bound, either by the makers or the public. Par otherwise was th• case. A tremen- dous amount of prejudice had to be broken down, and, as one of the earliest riders of the Rover type, I know how hard it was to convince people that it was a faster mount than the old high bicycle, as well as infinitely safer and better adapted to the varying requirements of old and young, strong and weak, expert and novice. Its riders were laughed at, described as

" BEETLES " AND " BLONDIN DONKEYS,"

and other strange things, for a very long time. They were debarred from races and tabooed generally. I remember attending a big meet of riders where, among some hundreds of machines ridden, my Rover was the only safety bicycle of that type in the crowd. . .

But conviction came at last to the majority of riders, and then people began to see the true possibilities of the wheel. Then it was that, iia addition to being a machine for pleasure and exercise, it first became a useful adjunct to the professional and business man.

It was long, however, before a woman was able to ride a salety bicycle iv/wont risaing a repuuatiou for "fastness." A few ladies, bolder than the bulk of their sisters, first made the plunge on bicycles specially built with low frames to accommodate their skirts. They met with a storm of disapproval even from cyclists, and for a time lady-cycling languished. It is difficult to say when public opinion definitely turned round and made it permissible for ladies to ride bicycles. When it did the fair sex seem to have entered the "biking " arena with an irresistible rush, and about three years ago they simply over- whelmed the manufacturers with their demands. To-day the output of bicycles for ladies must be quite equal to the number built for men, and the effect of this influx of the fair sex into cycling has been to elevate the pastime in a way that would have been deemed impossible a decade since. When cycling was confined to men, and mainly young men, cyclists! manners on the public roads were often marked by a boisterousness which brought the pastime into great disrepute. .....

Ten years ago a lady was only seen cycling on very rare occasions. When she did venture forth it was either on the front seat of a machine "built for two" or on a heavy single tricycle, and her mileage was not excessive under such circumstances. To-day our highways and byways are rendered delightful by the presence of the English girl cyclist, escorted by father, brother, or sweetheart. She has had a new world opened to her. Her ideas are expanding. She:has transformed cycling from a sport of athletic giants, eager to kill distance with humped back and hideous screech, into an element of everyday social life, and cycling is the better for her chastening influence.

In the early days the bicycle was frequently undergoing great changes of pattern, and very little special machinery had been invented to aid in its manufacture. Alterations in style followed each other so closely that bicycleAmilding was often a very risky enterprise financially. One or two radical changes have temporarily spelt disaster to numbers of makers,but have in each case eventually led to an enormous increase of trade. One of these radical departures was the present "Rover" type of bicycle, which left

===== 0002601_18980610_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
A 'CAST NUMBER OF lIIGH BICYCLES

on hand. Another was the sudden jump into popularity of the "Humber" shape of framework, with which we are all familiar, as it is that now universally adopted, and which made thousands of machines of the "Referee," or short-steering head type, almost unsaleable. The change, however, which created the most consternation among makers was the introduction of the air tyre. When it first came on the scene the manufacturers of cycles, after having made innumerable changes in their patterns, were con- gratulating themselves that at length "fixity of pattern" had been reached, and that for some years to come they could adopt one standard type and begin to recoup themselves for their years of experi- ment. With this idea in mind many of them had been building huge stocks of the then popular pattern, all made to suit the small solid rubber tyre, which was looked upon as perfect. For a time the leading manufacturers did all in their power to dissuade the public from ordering the new type of tyre, which soon proved itself so remarkably faster and more comfortable than the solid or "boot-lace " tyre. As well might Canute endeavour to keep back the waves. The pneumatic tyre had conic to stay, and had upset the calcula- tions of the greatest experts. Declared to be a fad and no improvement, it had effected as great a revolution in cycling as had the safety form of bicycle a few years before. Since its introduction, cycling has extended by leaps and bounds. The safety bicycle had opened the pastime practically to all, but the bogey of vibration still kept many aloof. The pneumatic tyre, reducing, as it did, vibration to a minimum, removed their objection. Since its advent there have been few, if any, changes in the bicycle. It has been improved in detail, but not substantially in design. Each year for the past four or five years, we have seen prac- tically the same pattern produced. The inevitable result has been to cheapen the cost of production, the necessity for constantly altering the tools and machinery, to produce the varying patterns which were necessary in the earlier days of the bicycle, being avoided. Automatic machinery has been introduced from America, and the whole tendency to-day is for bicycles to reach the great buying public at less and less cost. _ _ _

The fact that the present type of bicycle has now remained in a stationary condition for so long tempts one to the belief that it is practically as perfect as man can make it. So far as the present style is concerned, this is possibly the case. Before the bicycle can be considered a perfect motive machine, however, it must be so built that the rider is aided in the act of propulsion, and until the average man is enabled to cover a greater distance than is now possible, and with less muscular exertion, we can scarcely say that cycle invention has reached its climax. At any moment some new motive principle may upset all our existing ideas with regard to the pastime. Motor-cycling is in its earliest infancy yet.

A BULLET-PROOF' SOLDIER.

The " Scotsman " publi•-hes a letter written by Corporal Laurie,of the Seaforth Highlanders, to his sister,in which he gives an account of the battle of Atbara. "As for in% self," he remarks, "I suppose you have seen me reported as slightly wounded,and pictured me as an interested invalid. Well, I was struck,but the effect was so small that I have often been worse hurt in a football match. But during the rest of the day and for some days afterwards I have been an object of interest,and have been ques- tioned by almost every officer and man in the bat- talion, referred to as the ' bullet-proof man, and asked if I would mind letting a section fire a few rounds at me to see if they could do ma any harm. The fact is that I got most of the corners of my thothibg shot off, while personally I was uninjured. Before I entered the zareba I was not struck, but shortly afterwards a bullet took of the toe of my left shoe without hitting my foot, the shoe being a size too big,for easy marching and sleeping at night. They my bayonet was struck and bent ever at a right angle. Then a shot went through my sleeve, near my lett wrist tearing two holes but not hurting myself. Then my rifle was struck while I was load- ing, the bullet splintering the butt and being stopped by on irod bolt which it met. This bullet would certainly have gone right into my body but for my rill being there. Then a nigger ma trench let drive at me with a spear, missed my ribs by an inch, and slit up my haversack. A bullet then grazed the back of my hand, just enough to make it bleed. When I reached the river bank, which was nearly perpennicular, a shot came up from the bottom, abcut twenty feet below, and a little to the leftl which caused the wound I am supposed to have got and was so curious that I was paraded before the general. It entered through the lid of my right ammunition pouch, which was open, went into my right coat pocket, smashing a penknife and two pencils, tore four holes in my shirt, made a surface wound two or three inches long on my left breast, and came out near my left shoulder through my coat and ammuni I ion pouch braces. In the afternoon strolled over to the field hospital and got a piece of dressing on, and it has never troubled me at all ; in i fact t was a farce to put it in as a wound, and was done without my knowledge by the colour sergeant. So you see I've really been, as I'm told by every- body, wonderfully lucky in getting off as I have done, while other poor chaps were given no chances."

IT should not be hastily concluded The coal from the circumstances that that Strike. conference betweer the representa- tives of the mine owners and the men at Cardiff on Saturday came to an inconclusive termination, a serious deadlock prevailed in :the negotiations. 1 The Cardiff meeting was but the "first round" 4 in what may necessarily prove to be a prolonged , trial of tactical skill. The representative of tke: owners told the colliers' delegates that the tea , per cent. advance could not possibly be conceded, but that the owners were prepared to give an joy mediate advance of 3.16 per cent, above the wag° Paid during last March; This is not what had been expected, but it was better than nothing as an offer, and, since the miners and their dependaot!! have been suffering severe hardships during thell: period of enforced ioleness, it might }- ultimatum of the owners were noI rejected. on Tuesday, at which a full account of the pre- vious proceedings was submitted, but no point of agreement seems to have presented itself. So that the owners are again to be approached with the same terms as those already considered, and it is expected the meeting will be held to-day. At Tuesday's meeting the proposal to take a ballot of the miners with regard to having a sliding scale, as suggested by the owners, was rejected. The men's proposition was in two parts. The arst demanded an immediate advance of 10 per rent, and the second suggested .the creation of a ooneiliation board consisting of represen- tatives of capital and labour, "an arbi- trator to be mutually agreed upon to decide in the event of the respective represen- tatives failing to agree." It is certain that the meis made this proposition in all good faith, and soPposed it to be fair, but in industries such as ?°4-naining, where hundreds of men are engaged In one mine and an advance of a shilling or two a Week to each man involves a vast annual outlay, may not be unreasonable for those colliery pro- Prietors who are already forced to be content with small percentage on their capital expenditure to be very averse to placing it in the power of an ar- bitrator, however fair-minded he may be, to de- termine that they shall carry on their works at a rate that is unremunerative. The only safe rule 13/1 which such an arbitrator as the men propose could act would be to follow the selling price of oral, and to give his decision in accordance with that price at the time the dispute was referred to That is to say, Ile would have to set up a eliding scale in order to give his decisions; and sauce this is so, the question arises whether it would not be better for both parties that a new Sliding scale should be definitely recognised. The delegates who assembled on Tuesday appear to be absolutely opposed to such a plan, and if the men themselves are so, it becomes clear that in seine Other way wages will have to be governed by Prices, and there is no escape from such a conclu- sion. If the rate of wages is not governed auto- matically by the price of coal, then it will have to be settled from time to time by the acceptance or refusal of the demands made by one side or the Other, according as trade is slack or the reverse. Whether a sliding scale be formally recognised or in such as this there has to be a sliding scale. tIJ ie discussing economic questions to 01k, of what ought to happen ;it is impossible to .inske the facts square with theories. Wisdom lies ln knowing and recognising what does happen, and 1U making the best of well ascertained condi- tions.

' A, strange death occurred in a swirnmink b stl at Cheltenham last week, which should be Warning to bath keepers all over the country. ;lie victim of the fatality was a lad of ae, who was disporting himself in the Corporation baths, and in the course of his gaiabols he dropped from a swinging trapeze and disappeared. Toe mystery was soon Solved, and a terrible solution it was. The boys Companions found that the valve in the bottom of the bath for emptying purposes had been opened, and the suction of the outflowing water had drawn the boy so far in,that only his head and one ar. in were visible above the mouth of the pipe. The inquest resulted in the caretaker being censured, as the valve had been opened an hour before the advertised time for closing tho bath. In connec- tion with this sad affair we recollect on one occa- sion som3 time ago being in the Warrington C,)r- Poration plunge bath when the plug, or whatever contrivance was in use, had been removed, and Mr) Water was then pouring out.ln its egress it exerted ti suction force which one might easily imagine to he sufficient to overwhelm a youth whose swimming Powers were not very great. At the time there was only some four feet of water in the bath,which lessened what might have been a very serious danger. Seeing what a sad result has followed a little unusual haste in empting the bath at Cheltenham, it behoves all bathkeepers to be ex- teedingly cautious in opening the egress valve, which may become such a real and imminent danger to 'bathers. -- Again, the proceedings at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council have been devoid °f that kilocorum which should, indisputably, be ”sociated with them. Those active and occasion- ally peace-disturbing little bodies which came into existence with the passing of the Parish Councils Act, have .established a title to "liveliness" now generally admitted, but it is expected that the aasembliesof such august bodies as Town Councils Will conduct their deliberations with some res- .act for dignity and propriety. was originally intended, that the old river bed was a nuisance and injurious to health, and that the possibility of it being available in the construction of docks was so remote, as not to be worthy of consideration. On the other hand it was a cour- teous way of meeting the Ship Canal Company, far preferable to bleeding them, and he had hopes of gaining the Company's consent to Warrington tradersentering or leaving the canal at any point between Warrington and Eastham. Such is the sum total of Alderman Roberts' lucid arguments in support of the recommendation. The only out- and-out opponent ef it was Councillor Bennett, who discoursed at great length on what might be, chiefly arguing that the river bed would be of use for dockage accommodation, and severely rating the Corporation for letting things slide and failing to take advantage of the Ship Canal's proximity. There is yet, we fear, too much of the " dreamer " about Councillor Bennett—he looks too far in the future. It would be the height of folly to leave the river bed in its present open state on the very intangible ground that it might be available for the construction of docks. There is not the least probability that there will even be the need for docks for some years to come, and to adopt such a conrse would not only be an injustice to the residents on its banks but a piece of folly which might have grave and far- reaching effects on the health of the town. More- over, should the docks come to be constructed here,which is not the present plan,the river course could easily be dredged out again at compara- tively small cost. Councillor Tinnion appeared to think the Corporatio9 were letting the Canal Company off too easily,and urged that something more should be got from them if they were re- lieved of their obligation to construct a penstock. The privilege ot entering or leaving the Canal at any point between. N'k, arrington and Eastham would probably satisty .Councillor Tinnion's ac- quisitiveness. A. reduction of the tolls would be a boon almost too much to hope for, and might increase the usefulness of the lay-by, which was stated to be boycotted because of the excessive dues. There were only three dissentients from the resolution,in carrying which the Council have taken a big step towards doing away with a nuis- ance which the Medical Officer has condemned in the strongest terms.

Tha amount of alcohol in many so-called teeto- tal drinks has been the subject of much comment during the past twelve months. Fresh evidences of the heady and alcoholic nature of these popular drinks are almost daily adduced in the Police Courts up and down the country. Morally inno- cent shopkeepers find themselves victimised by the pernicious character of beverages they retail in the belief that they are non-alcoholic. A wide- awake inspector comes in with the utmost friend- liness, purchases a bottle of herb beer, hop ale, or some such liquid delicacy designed for the teetotal palate,and departs. Shortly after the seller of the liquor receives a summons based on the ille- gality of its containing more than 2 per cent.proof spirit and is mulcted in some more or less ade- quate penalty. It is surprising what a quan. - tity of alcohol is occasionally found in these deceitful decoctions. Why I there are many varieties of mild beer, sold as such, con- taining a less percentage of the spirit which the teetotaler so greatly abhors. A recent case at Scarborough disclosed analyses of hop ale contain- ing 2.3 per cent of proof spirit; hop bitters 4.9 per cent; and hop stout 6 per cent. Really it is wrong, very wrong,that the staunch blue-ribbonite should be laid open to the risk of infringing those prin- ciples which he has so arduously maintained for may-be a lifetime. After all, water seems to be the o_.ly species of liquid refreshment which is safe from toe odium of reproach on one ground or another.

It seems impossible for Voluntary School Managers, in Warrington at least, to agree about anything. The latest fiasco is in reference to the school holidays. A few weeks ago it was decided at a meeting of managers and teachers called by the School Attendance Committee that the sum- mer holidays for the elementary day school scholars should commence on July 15th and thus extend over August Bank Holiday week. Another proposal has since been brought forward by the Rector for the schools to close on the day preced- ing Walking Day and to re-open on the Tuesday, following August Bank Holiday. If this course is adopted most scholars will practically have a si x weeks holiday for it is only reasonable to expect that many children will not attend regularly dur- ing the. "breaking up" week when school will only be open until Thursday, and it is still less probable that they will promptly return to school after so universal a holiday as Bank Holiday. It was to prevent this falling off in theattendance and consequent loss to the schools, and also to save the extra labour thrown upon the School Attendance officers, that the arrangement was made to com- mence the holidays on July 15th. Whether man- agers of other schools will accept the suggestion of the Rector or remain faithful to their own arrangement remains to be seen. The Catholic schools, we have reason to believe, intend to be included amongst the faithful.

Our local " defenders" are to be congratulated upon the plucky way with which they have stuck to their duty at the encampment last week. The inconveniences and hardships they endured dur- ing the early part of the week, due to the March- like gales and rains which were experienced, were sufficient to try well-seasoned soldiers, inured to camp life and with the incentive of actual warfare. The failure or success of an encampment largely depends upon the weather, but despite the un- favourable conditions from Sunday until Thurs- day morning a good week's training has been put in. The health of the men has been very satisfac- tory on the whole, and it is to be hoped that they will not suffer on their return, from any afte6 effects of "roughing it" at Rhyl. Rarely has i been their lot before to be subjected to so much damp with so little effective shelter. The tents have not been as waterproof as they might have been, but as they are supplied by the War Depart- ment, the local officers cannot be blamed. Every effort was made on their part to make the men comfortable, and if they were not entirely success- ful in this direction it must be put down to the tremendous odds against them.

A " living wage," the only remuneration for la- bour which can be at all fair and adequate does not appear to be paid even by the Governtnent, which can surely afford to give its employees a reasonable weekly pay. '4 The tale comes from Portsmouth that in connection with the telegraph service there, the Department has just taken on two female and one male employee;in view of the extra pressure during the summer months. Their I"salaries" are 14s. and 16s. per week, respectively, and they do the work of operators. We do not know the exact qualifications which a person must have to undertake this work, but we are sure that 16s. a week is very poor remuneration for it. One of the girls who has secured this "good thing" at 14s. weekly, had exceeding Igreat difficulty in finding lodgings, and ultimately when an abode was secured she had to pay exactly 'the same sum per week as she received from the Department as "salary." What she will do to secure any little luxury—nay necessity—in the shape of clothes, washing, or extra food, is a problem which cannot be solved on 14s. a week. It is a matter for great regret that competition is so keen as to compel people to work at such star- vation wags, and nothing short of a scandal that the Government will take advantage of this hardness of the times in the way it seems to be doing. NARROW EECAPE FROM DROWNING AT RUNCORN. On Saturday afternoon a boy named Sherlock nar- rowly escaped drowning at the clay pit off Norman-road. It transpires that the little fellow was playing on the bank at the side of the water when he accidentally over- balanced himself and fell into the pit. The water was rather deep at this particular spot, and seeing the dan- ger he was in, a playmate went in search of his father, who, happily, was in the vicinity at the time, and arrived on tee scene just as his son W 49 going down. He rushed into the water and brought the little fellow ashore. Be was then taken home, where he remained unconscious for some time, but eventually recovered from the effects of immersion. TELE GarrEsoum SPECTACLE AT RUNCORN—THE BODY

IDENTIFIED. Oa Saturday morning last the body of the young man found in the Top Locks basin was identified as that of John Thomas Mather, butcher, of Flixton, Manchester, son of a cotten dealer of the same address. He was last seen at home on Friday the 27th of May. Tnere is no cause to indicate how the deceesed became drowned and although his watch and chain have been missed, it is undoubtedly due to the fact that the remains of the young fellow were horribly mutilated and turned about by the propellers of passing tugs.—Mr. J. E. Worsley held an inquest on Friday when evidence was given by Fred Crompton, 20 Vine-street, and P.C. Gregory of the way in which the body was recovered.—A verdict of " found drowned" was returned. A MONTI/ FOR AN IMPUDENT BEGGAR.

John Reid, a respectably-attired man, describing him- self as a clerk out of employment, was charged at Widnes on Monday with begging on Saturday, William Horner,a resident in Deacon-road, said that the prisoner called at his house on Saturday, and after boldly walk. log past the servant into the parlour asked witness for assistance, at the same time spinsing a pitiful yarn of poverty. Witness gave the prisoner a shilling as rail- way fare to Liverpool Reid then left the house. Mr. Horner afterwards saw the prisoner talking to a lady in Albert-road, and, becoming suspicious, he informed the police, and the prisoner was apprehended. On the way to the police station he remarked to Police-constable Gibson, " The man who gave the information against me can only get me a month." The prisoner, who pleaded guilty, said that be was a native of Duudee,and had been travelling about the county for the past twelve months. He was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour. A VICIOUS HORSE IN PERCIVAL-STREET.

An accident that might have been attended with more serious results occurred in Percival-street on Tues- day morning. John White, of the London and North- Western Railway goods yard, . was taking a horse belonging to the company down to the smithy, when it became restive at sight of a man suddenly coming out of Mr. Abel's store- room, threw White to the ground striking him on the shoulder and hand whilst on the ground. White, how- ever, got on his feet and pluckily attempted to get hold of the horse to preYent it doing further mischief, when theanimal reared up and brought its foreleg with great force upon White's face inflicting a severe wound to the eye brow. Although bleeding profusely the man stuck to the animal and succeeded in taking it to the smithy after which be went to Dr McDougal who found it neces- sary to put four stitches in the wound to draw it to- gether. It is surprising that the young man epoaped so easily, as when upon the ground he was entirely at the mercy of the animal, but it is gratifying to note he iS now progressing favourably. THE GUARDIANS AND THE !NEW) VACCINATI N BILL. Mr. Mclntyre reported that tue Parliamentary Corn- mittee met that morning, prior to the Board Meeting, to consider the clauses of the Vaccination Bill now before I Parliament. as many of them were aware the Bill had been read a second time, and if they intended moving in ' the matter, they would have to be quick about it. As a result of their deliberations the Committee recom- mended that the Board adopt the following resolution : , "That this Board is most strongly of opinion that clause 3 of the Bill now before the House of Commons entitled, "A Bill to amend the law in respect to vaccinatioo is a direct infringement of the liberties of the people and also of the liberties and powers which sholtd be exorcised by Poor Law Guardians of this enintry; and therefore expresses its strong and emphatic disapproval of such proposed le,rislatian; this Board also respectfully but firmly calls upon the Howe of Com- mons to refuse its sanction to this or any similar measure unless provision is math' in the Bill that any rules and regulations thereby proposed to be made by the Local Government Board, shall not come into operation until they have been laid upon the table of the House of Com- mons 40 days' and have received the sanction of the Efoos ; and that a copy of this resolution be forwarded to the Parliamentary representatives who have constitu- ents in this union."—Clauee 3of the Bill read as fol- lows : "Me Local G.)vernment Board shall hav the same powers of making rules and revilations with re- pect to public vaccination whether under contracts made before or after the passing of this Act. as they have with f respect to vaccination officers, and any rules aed regulations made by the B lard with respect COvv.cirination whether under this or any. 0:tor Act shall while in force have effect as if enacted by Otis Act." .

The Bill simply now prop-m.ld to give the whole of the powers to the Local Goverment Board who would dictate to the Board of Guardians. THE SANI2ARY CONDITION OF THE RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT.

There is just to band ths report of Dr. Joseph Adams, MR., CM., (Medical Officer of Health to the Runcorn District Council) upon the sanitary condit.iou of the dis- trict of the Runcorn Rural Districr Council. The report elves a detailed acconnt with statistical tables of the sickness and mortality within On district, classified according to diseases, ages and localities; a summary of the actions taken during the year for the prevention of the spread of disease ; an account of the inquiries testi.. toted, and of the sapervision exercised by tie Medical Officer as to the conditions injurious to health in the die- trict, together with the manner of dealing with the SAM" ; and also a record of the action advised by the Medical Officer in regard to toe treatment of offensive trades, factories and workshops, and for the regulation of dairies, covrsherls, and milk shops. Glancing briefly through the report we see that the Runcorn Rural District includes 39 townships with 49 309 acres, and an estimated population of 26,737 (estimated to the middle of 1897). During the year end- ling December 31st, 1897, 613 births and 343 deaths were segistesed,as haying occurred within the district ; these figures cornosred with 1893 show the remarkable de- crease of 119 births, and an increase of 21 deaths. De. ducting the number of deaths which occurred in Dutton Workhouse, known to belong to other unions, however, gives an emended total of 330 deaths of which 31 I. armed the suhject of Coroner's ih.quente, and no less than /4 were "uncertified," and no inquest was held. Then fol. lows a table cif the nature of death and reason tor ths Coroner'a inquest:—Burned, 3; epilePsx- 1: found I drowned, 8; natural causes, 11 ; overl4io,l ; run over, 4'. found dead, 2; poisoned by entering a sewer (employers censued),l ; total,3l.—Of the 613 births 311 were males,

I and 302 females; whilst the deaths include 189 males and 154 females for the whole, and 183 males and 147 females for the corrected total exclusive of foreign cases. Contrasted with several previous years, the total deaths from all zymotic diseases were only 26 I compared wi:h 42 in 1896, typhus fever which occurred in 1886.tnade no reappearance, nor was there any traces of smallpox, indeed the report goes on to state " every zymotic disease was at a very low ebb as to death rate," as the following table will show I.—Smallpox 0 ; scarlet fever, 7 ; diptheria 3 ; croup, 1 ; typhus 0 ; typhoid fever 1 ; continued fever, 0 ; relapsing fever. 0 ; puce- Petal, 0 ; cholera, 0 ; ervsipelas, 0 ; measles, 2 ; whoop- ing cough, 8 ; diarrhoei 3 ; rheumatic fever,l ; total 26. Influenza is described as having left its " calling card" upon tbe British nation and Dr. Adams con- tends it should rank in company with cholera, staallpox, typhoid fever aid other " infeetioua I dignitaries." Since 1890, when influenza first reappeared in annual returns, after an absence of about 50 years; it has accounted for the following deaths per year in the Rnocorn District in 1890. 7 deaths; 1891,19 deaths;lB92, 17 deaths; 1893, 7 deatne;lB94,l2 deaths; 1895,12 deaths; 1896, none; whilst in 1897 it rose to 10 deaths. Of other diseases, phthisis caused 26 death.' ; bronchitis, pneu- monia ana pleurisy,6o deaths ; heart disease, 34 deaths; injuries, accident and violence, 17 deaths; other diseases I 170 deaths. In dismissing this section the report goes on to state that taken on the whole it is one of the most favoureble which it has ever been the pleasure of the compiler to present. Accompanying the report are four appendix' (1 and 2) of statistical tables of mortality aad inlenous d,seases o7.curricg within the Runcorn Rural Dirtrict during the year 1897; (3) of the water analysis e :inducted by the County Analyst, in 1897 and (4) a summary of water analysis arranged in townships from the year 1879 to 1897. The whole report is brim-full of interesting informa- tion, and is compiled with Dr. Adams' usual methedical ability,ancl will uadoubted'y be perused with zest by al! interested in the health of Ituncern and district.

itENCOI6 NOTES. [BY A CORRESPONDENT]. The ancients—who were really and truly philoso- phers—hand down to us the legend "to break a bard stone, 'tie best to keep on knocking it," which is to- day as much a truism as it was then. Therefore it is reasonable to suppose that if one would secure a conees.ion in the way of an improvement of any kind. a little persistent perseverance is all that is required to bring it about. Again if persistency suc- ceeds in the breaking of stones,tbe Runcorn Guar- dians (undoubtedly experienced enough in that re- spect) need not despair when they receive a slight reverse, as the one in the case of lighting of the Latchford Locks.

That a %umber of lives have been lost in the vicinity of these locks is common knowledge.but an analysis or the many cases,points—not so much to the want of better protection and lighting.for which the Ship Canal Company are asked—as to a want of just a little ordinary care which the general pub- lic might exercise when using the lock as a feet- way. A glance at the details of the sad fatality which occurred there on Monday week illustrates the case, and the jury might have refrained from magnifying the dangers of the locks, or at least re- quested the public to try to do their share by exer- cising a little more cxre,though after all "accidents" are accidents which happen by accident.

One effect of the matter being ventilated,however, will be that the Manchester Ship Canal Company will probably do their share—whether liable or not liable I feel sore is immaterial to them—in making the locks as secure as is compatible with their working.

There is another great nece-,sity, which must not be overlooked, and that is tl-ie acquirement of a suitable road from Runcorn to Weston Point. Weston Point is a growing place of no lit tie import- ance, and not a little of the profit of the Runcorn storekeeper comes from this becoming centre of the salt industry. All who have ever taken a walk to Weston Point must be well aware of the existence of that spot called the "death-trap" in the vicinity of the Weston Point locks; and there are not a-few who remember it but too well. Here there iSa very narrow pe,h, and if land cannot be obtained to widen it, surely permii;sion might be obtained to erect a handrail, sufficient to protect anyone from falling into the water. This consummation might ' be accomplished by any public body, company, or authority, and is one which all of them might well take a hand in from merely humanitarian prin- ciples.

Dr. McDougall ought,and is to be highly oomph- mented on the slccess of his scheme tor the t c with- don of a county laboratory for the chemical and bacteriological testing of articles of food— especially of milk and water. True, the County Council have declined to found such an institution, but in the same breath they virtually admit the necessity of it. They say make use of the existing facilities at Liverpool and Manchester. A remark- able concession for a County Council to make, but nevertheless a concession. They admit its need— they admit what Dr. McDougall pointed out. Now it will only be a matter of time before the county will be indebted to Rancorn and its Medical Offlcer of Health, for an institution which cannot but con- tribute to the weal of all the inhabitants.

BOLD-STREEr WESLEYAN CiiITELCJI.

ANNIVERSARY SERVICES AND LECTURE. The anniversary services in connection with the Bold. street Wesleyan Sunday School, were held on Sunday. At the morning and evening services the Bey. John Bond, of London, preached to large congregations. In the afternoon an interesting address was given by the Rev. W. Wood of Sheffield, special music was sung by the choir and scholars at each service. The collection during the day amounted to £37. On Monday evening the Rev. J. Bond, tz svC an address on " Cromwell and the Puritans." The chair was taken by Mr. It. G. Henshall. There was only a fair attendance. The lecturer took his audience to the time of the Reformation, which he said, spraag from two sources :—From political necessity and the long felt need of an ignorant people. He described the execution of Hooper, and touched upon some of the chief incidents of the time of Elizabeth and James. Oliver Cromwell, he said, was a man a little above the middle stature with a very harsh voice. Cromwell went to school and later to the University of Canforid4e. where be acquired only an ordinary acquaintance of knowledge. Presently he got married and settled down as a farmer,'and passed through that stage which brought him before us as a sedate and thoughtful man. Crom- well later went into Parliament. He took up the reins of Government as they fell from incompetent bands and immediately set himaelf against King Charles. After the breaking up of the Parliament, a notice was found chalked upon one of the doors one morning which said " This house to let unfurnished." That the lecturer said was probably true. Cromwell. eventually redormed the Parliament which made him protector. Cromwell was a match for all political trouble, tut fin- ally domestic trouble came which quite overcame him. His daughter was taken ill and despite all endeavonrs she died. Cromwell died soon after leaving, a character to his country never to he forgotten. The lecturer touched upon the character of the Puritans whom some pecple thought were hypocrites. This was not true,they were anything but that, though they had their faults. The regiment of Ironsides which Cromwell raised amongst the farmers of Eseex to fight aoeinst the Parlia- mentary forces were examples of the Puritans of that time. They were men of stern, unbending character. This was proved by the fact that they were never beaten. The Ironsides were net made out of hypocrites nor could hypocrites be made out of " Ironsidee." Cromwell could not be a hypocrite to inspire all these men. No man was a successful hypocrite in his own family for long. The only thing Cromwell's daughter thought would do her any good was his presence. Cromwell was to hi? daugh- ter the tenderest nurse that all the wealth of England could produce.9e was a man of earnestness and religion. The lecturer pointed out that from the puritans sprang come of the greatest men the world has ener known. Such men as John Bunyon, John Hemoden, and John Milton. who were the Puritan Palm ;eves," and amongst them stood that " rough oak" Oliver Crom- well. Twe Stuarts preceded and two Stuarts succeeded Cromwell. Cromwell wee a man of very great common sense, and ho never allowed himgelf to be carried away by theories. The lecturer shortly afterwards concluded what had proved to be a most iuteresting and instructive address. A vote of thanks was pessed to tho lecturer, and to Mr. Henshall for taking the chair.

BEFORE THE MA.GISTRATES.

A BATCH OF UNLICENSED _LODGING HOUSE CASES,

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday morning, before the Mayor and other magistrates. Hannah Ward, 1, Alder-lane ; Edward Kilroy, 3, Alder- lane ; John Adams, 315, Winwick-road ; Henry Craven, 323, Winwick-road ; Peter Maguire, 297, Winwick-road; and Joseph Williams, 295, Winwicit•road, were all summoned at the instance of Inspector Norman with keeping lodgers without having their houses regis- tered.—lnspector Norman stated that all the defendants lived at the North end of the town, and they were accommodating lodgess who worked at Winwick Asylum. The defendant were unable to make proper accommo- dation for their families owing to overcrowding by lodgers. There was plenty of room for lodgers at the registered lodging houses. Is one case thirteen persons were found in a house only adapted for six. Unless a stop were put to It they would have a great deal of trouble with these people during the construction of Winwick Asylum.—lnspector Powell gave evidence as to visiting the defendants houses. There was more or less overcrowding in every house, and none of the defen- dants were registered. In Craven's house there were eleven persons on the 25th ult where there was only accommodation for six ; and in Kilray's house eleven persons were found living there, when there should only have been six.—The magistrates fined Craven and Kil- ray 7s. each, and the other defendants 3s 6d. each. They were ordered to get without their lodgers or to have their houses registered.

TIIIIRSDAY.—Before Mr. Chandley (In the chair), Alderman Sutton, and Mr. Hepherd. "HIS FIRST LAPSE "—THEFT OF BOOTS.—A lad named Herbert Wright, said to be 12 years of age. was charged with stealing a pan* at boots the property of the Bankruptcy Boot Stores. 48. Horsemarkkt-street. The lad pieaded guilty.— The Chief Constable said this was a very painful case indeed. The defendant had been for some time in the employ of the firm as errand boy, and had previously borne a very good character. en Wednesday afternoon two boys went into a pawnshop in Winwick-road.and pledged the pair of boots in question. The assistants lathe shop did not Ilice the look of things and questioned the lads,and in the meantime sent for the police, but the lads ran away. P.C. Edwards was called in, and Ise made inquiries into the matter, which led him to pay a visit to the st.sres in Hersernarket-street, where he saw the defendant. He asked him if he had attempted to pawn a pair of boots. He •at first denied that he had done so. bat subsequently admitted his guilt, and said he stole the boots from Inside the shop on Monday night. The gentleman who had pre.ferred the charge against the lad was anxious the Bench should deal with him as leniently as possible ; In fact he was anxims for the charge to be withdrawn alto- gether. but he was told that that could not be allowed.—The defendant. whase age it now transpired was 13, said they rubbed the boots in the dust to make them look old.—Mr. Chandley severely lectured the boy upon his bad conduct.and also gatre him some excellent advioe as to his future beha- viour. The lad said be was sorry, and the Bench ordered him to be kept in the Bridewell until six o'clock in the even ing.—lt was stated that the lad's employers were willing t- take him again. • . .

UNRITLY'bRILDREN*—WiIIiam Ellis, of Thewll,-:treet, was charged with tailing to send his son George to school regu- larly. He now asked for him to be sent to the High Town Truant School, Liverpool, until he was 14 years of age The Bench decided to adopt that course.—Bdward Smith, of 72, Cartwright-street, was similarly charged. Mr. Jones said the case was before the Bench on duesday. The lad would not attend any school and was continually on the streets during the day, and he slept out during the night. The father said he coald do no good with the lad. For three weeks he took him to school every morning, but he would not g • in the afternoon because he (the father) could not look after him, as he was at his work. He was up till three o'clock that morning looking after him. The uhairman : What have you dune to him? The father: I have panished him In many ways, but it Idoes him no good. I have six other children, but I have no trouble with them.- It was decided to send the lad to Ard- wick Green School, Manchester. until he was IS years of age.

Herr Melander finds that the number or dust particles in the air increases with its dryness, and that there I' usually a minimum in the afternoon, Land winds carry more dust then sea winds. Win ts blowing out of an anticyclone or down from the mountains are very dusty. Products of com- bustion furnish a portion of dust to the air.

The polype is one of the most remarkable creatures known . If cut transversely or longitudinally into several parts each will become a perfect animal. The naturalist Trembly turned them inside out and yet they ate and seemed to enjoy them- selves as much as ever. He slit two longitudinally, placed the halves together and united them into two new animals ; he divided two transversely and crested one with two heads; he pushed ens down the throat of another, a third down the throat of the second and this formed a creature with three heads.

On Sunday morning. as !)river Franks we taking the first train from Shoeburyness to Southend. he noticed a gentle- man walking along the line. When the train was nearly upon tim, the gentleman, with head erect and looking straight at, the engine, coolly stepped on the track, and the wh-de train pissed over him. The body, which was terribly mangled, was suhs,•quently identified as that of William Henry Fisher. 2, Oxford-terrace. Southend-on-Sea, a com- mercial traveller, and on the staff of the trade journal Timber."

The largest fireproof Curtain yet constructed for a theatre hasjust been fitted at Drury-lane by Merryweather and Sons. It measures 421 t by 30ft. 61n., and is suspended by steel wire ropes running over pulleys fixed above the proscenium open- ing, counterbalance weights being provided so that it can be easily raised or lowered by one man. Should the wire ropes break through fire or accident, the curtain which is entirely of iron and asbestos, and weighs several tons, is prevented from falling suddenly by means ot a pair of pneumatic cylin• dere. which check tin descent. Gladstone and Disraeli will henceforth stand opposite in the Abbey as they were rivals in Parliament, and this tins,' and parr/lament propinquity but points and preserves the piquancy of the eontrist between their characters and careers Gladstone published " Juventus Mundt " In 1869, and in 1870 appeared Disraeli's " Lothair." The two authors were both at the larsig'it of their political rivalry and renown, and John Tenniel put them together in a cartoon as critics of each others books— as "critics who had not exaetly failed in literature and art " Gladstone's criticism ofi "fothalr " Is " Flippant and Disraeli's on " Juventus Mundt," "Ha I Prosy." The Fairfield Shipbuilding Company ,Glasgow, successfully launched on Saturday the second-class cruiser Hightlyer for the British Government. The vessel is an improvement on the Juno class, and having engines of 10,000 horse power her speed will be twenty knots. Tile Hight! ver is of 5,600 tons displacement, and is suecially constructed for long for- eign cruises. She is built throughout with Siemens steel,and has a cellular bottom extending the full length of the engine - room and boiler space The cruiser has also a powerful ram, and is fitted with a bilge keel to secuna steadiness in firing, Belleville boilers are used, and two sets of triple expansion_ enoines hove been placed on board. The length of the High flyer is 350 ft by 54. Her armament consists of eleven 6in. guns, eight 12-pounders, Maxims, and submerged tarpedo nty,

THE SCHOOL HOLIDAYS AT WARRINU TON.

RUPTURE IN TEIE ARRANGEMENTS.

Having heard a rumour that the arrangement come to at the meeting of managers and teachers With reference to a uniform holiday for the elemen- tary day school scholars was likely to fall through, a representative of the " Examiner " called upon Councillor Shaw-Green, the Chairman of the School Attendance Committee, to ask if there was any troth in it. Mr. Shaw-Green said that neither he nor any member of the Committee had had any communication of an official character with reference to it, but the R. Father Wilson had called upon him and informed him that he had re- ceived a communication from the Rector of War- rington asking him to agree to an alteration of the date agreed upon at the meeting. Father Wilson informed Mr Shaw Green that he and his conferers had determined to stick to the date arranged at the meeting for the commencement of the summer holidays. viz. : July 15th. We understand, on re- liable authority, that it is proposed to commeace the holidays in the Church of England schools on the day before Walking Day and continue until the day after August Bank holiday. This will mean that these schools will be closed for 41 weeks. The arrangement made at the meeting in the Town Hall a month ago was that the holidays should uni- formly commence on the 15th of July.

TRU REMOVAL OF NIGHT SOIL.

One of the members said the night soil was not being removed till 9 or 10 o'clock in the morning.—The Chair. man remarked that many compaints bad been made and it was a very common occurence for the night soil not to be removed until that time. _ _ _

After some discussion it was moved and seconded that the Clerk should write to the contractor Mr. New- ton, and let him know that the night soil was not being removed at the time specified.

TKZ _BATHS OF STOCHTON MUTH.

The Clerk said he had received the following letter from Mr. F. Pearson :—Dear sir,—" The borough rate of Stockton Heath is getting so high that there is a igeneral desire to know how it is arrived at. At the next I regular parochial meeting I shall be glad if you will be prepared to tell me how the figures are arrived at. The Clerk's reply was as follows : The calls for Stock- ton Heath for the present half-year are as follows : Poor rate, £B4 ; county rate, £8; district rate sanitary ex- penses, £23 ; highway exp rises, £l3l ; Parish Council, £4O ; voter's list,ete., £3O ; total, £3Bl. It was estim- ated the rate would make £425, which would leave a balance at the end of the year of £44. The assessable value of Stockton Heath at Lady Day was £8,611 10s., and you will therefore see that the calls reads upon tha parish only ansount to a very little over 111, in the £. The calls made upon Walton Inferior amounted in all to £159, and the assessable value to £3,571 10i. being just under 11d. in the £. The call made upon the parish of Runcorn Union was only (includinc county rate)£l,634. The assessable value was £5,477 Bs. It all there- fore amounted to just over 7d. in the Z. You may therefore see that if the rates have been increased in the parish it was not through any increased call made by the Guardians or the Rural District Council. I rather think the true reason is that the balance in the hands of the Overseers had got down to vanishing point, and the new Assistant Overseer is trying to repair it. lam 1 writing to the Assistant Overseer for a copy of his de- mand note, and I will lay full particulars before the Parish Council at their next inee' ing. If you require any further information I shall he glad to furnish it. You will of course notice that I have not included the special sanitary call in the foregoing particulars, This I presume you did not require, as it is a special charge on the Parish and is not included in the same demand. —Yours truly, John Ashten (clerk).

THE TIME OF MEETING.

Mr. Ashton Hayes said that at the last meeting he pro- posed that the meetings should be held in future at a quarter to six, as it seemed to suit the majority. He nroposed as an amendment that the meeting be held at 6 o'clock. Mr. Pilling seconded and the motion was carried. This was all the business.

INEXPLICABLE INCAPACITY.

SIR CHARLES DILIKE CONDENINS TORY POLICY. Sir Robert Reid, Q.C., M.P., as president of the Dearl Forest Liberal Association, gave his address on Thursday last week at CinderforcLand dealt with foreign affairs. Sir Charles Dilke. M.P., moved the vote of thanks, which was seconded by Sir William Wedderburn, 01. P. Sir Charles Dilke, criticising the annual report of the association, which discussed the Liberal leadership, said that a baseless paragraph had suggested that he was tak- iny part in such discussions His own attitude in July 1901 would be the same as it had been in 1874, when the question had arisen on Mr. Gladstone's first retirement. In 1880, when it had been desired to sobsti- tote Mr. Gladstone for Lord Hartington, and in 1894,0 n Mr. Gladstene's final retirement, he, as a Radical, took no part in the choice,and gave in home affairs a general support to the person chosen so long and so far as he pursued a Radical policy. As regards Army reform and the sufficiency of our fleets, he had a policy of his own. In foreign affairs he was always prepared to support the national policy apart from party ties,but it must be con- sistent and firm in order to be peaceful. The late Cabinet had made one curious and unexplained blunder in their Congo Convention, but the present Government had committed every blunder that lay open to them. A great merchant had written to him that day : " Why not have sold out the whole stock-in-trade and goodwill of the British Empire before both had be- come too badly depreciated ?" In 1895 he had hoped to be able to support the Salisbury Ad minis- tration in foreign affairs, as in Army reform, but they had displayed inexplicable incapacity at every point. If they were yielding everywhere, why in their speeches did they frighten us to death? That they did not mean business was clear when they reduced the tobacco duty instead of building ships. Their conduct of foreign affairs was universally condemned, and their military policy only needed to be as widely understood to stand subject as a general a censure, while in Army matters they had failed to use an opportunity which might never return. It was now certain that they would be in a minority in July 1901, but if Liberals did not think out in advance the problems which would then confront them, they would not be able to use their chances.

CHEMICAL WORKS IN LANCASHIRE The report of the chief inspector of alkali, &c., works was issued last Thursday. Altogether there are 1,058 registered works in England, Ireland, and Wales, or 16 fewer than in the preceding year. The visits pail by the inspectors numbered 4,116, and the number of testS made by them was 4,562. The only prosecution under- taken in Lancashire was against a Miles Platting firm, who, for failing to use the best means for preventing escape of noxious gases in the manufacture of nitric acid, were fined £2O. The old qtestion of nuis• ancys around Widnes and St. Helens is again ' revived, the chief inspector remarking that complaints of nuisances arising, from the old deposits of alkali waste are received from time to time, these complaints chiefly relating to the drainage or these heaps at Widnes, and to the sewers to the tidal estuary of the Mersey, while but little improvement is to no noted in the condition of Sankey Brook. The operations at the large works of the Castner-Kellner Company at Runcorn are specially mentioned. The in- spector general says it was not found posAble to induce the men to work the chambers as they are worked in Germany, the men professing that it was necessary to get into the chambers—which were only three feet high —on their hands and knees, to get out the bleach, and clean out the corners. Eventually the chambers were rebuilt, and raised to the normal height of 6ft. 61n. The inspector mentions that an excellent precaution- ary measure is adopted by the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company, " who desire to show that in this special chemical operation they could provide that their work people should not suffer in any degree,and who, Mr. W. Mather informs me, have ehgageci the professional ser- vices of a local doctor, who attends the works regularly' to look at the workpeople and the conditions under which they labour, and who is obliged to communicate with the firm in writing every fortnight his opinion as to whether in every detail the people are working under conditions which are in no way injurious to health. Thiel is of special importance, seeing that in the cell roons considerable quantities of mercury are regularly in the charge of the cell attendants, and no evil effects have been observed."

THE HYDE CASE.

THREE TIMES REPORTED DEAD. A CORRESPONDENT of the" Ashton Reporter " was der puted quite recently by the editor of his journal to in- vestigate a case at Hyde, Cheshire, the circumstances Of which gave promise of much interest. He proceeded to 83, Manchester-road, Hyde, and entering the shop, en• quired for Mr. Edward Wood. A hearty "Good morn. ing " attracted his attention to a sturdy old gentieman standing by the counter. "You are surely not Mr. Edward Wood, who ha. recently had a severe attack of paralysis r' "I am," be replied," though you're not the fire that's been surprised." And this, in his own intelligent words, is the story thB old gentleman then related :

I

" I was 75 years of age last August. Per some years when living at Rochdale, I suffered from sciatica, and doctors attonded me, but I became worse instead of better. In despair I ceased to take their physic. when a young man advised me to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I did so, and soon began to feel better•—• in fact, after taking five boxes I threw my sticks awaY• and pronounced myself cured. I fairly danced:with jel for I was able to get back to my work after having beea laid up helpless for months. We then removed to Hyde, and my health kept good until November last, when I had a stroke of paralysis. which rendered my limbs useless. I lay like a log in bed for ovnr a month, and the physic I took gave me no re- lief. I felt myself gradually sinkidg,when my wife said : "We'll try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills again." She got a box and in legs than a fortnight I felt much stronger, while after a month had elapsed I was ahle to get out of bed and use my limbs. A. day or so -later I came downstairs and got about the house. That was the result of seven boxes. Since then I have been able to walk up to the barber's, and I help in the shop again now. I regard my cure as miraculous, for I never expected to come downstairs again. Why, it was reported three times that I was dead." "But tell me this. Are you firmly convinced that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have saved your life?" " I am," the old man replied," as surely as I believe soy name to he Edward Wood. Truly Mr. Wood's recovery seems like s miracle, but it is a miracle in which all can believe, because it has a scientific cause in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which by act- ing on the nervous system, went at once to the root of the mischief. They are a splendid nerve and spinal tonic, and thus have cured many cases of paralysis, locomotor ataxy, neuralgia, and nervous headache. They also cure rheumatism, sciatica, ansemia or hs• poverished blood, consumption, erysipelas, St. Vitus' dance, rickets, and indigestion. Tey are obtainable of all chemists, and from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company. 48. Holborn-viaduct, London, at 2s. 91. a box, or six for 13s. 91, but are genuine only when bearing the full name, Dr, Williams' Pink Pills for Pals People, printed n red on the pink wrapper.

FOOTBALL.

THE NORTHERN UNION. THE NEW SCHEME OF PROFESSIONALISM. The sub-committee of the Northern Roby Football Union entrusted with the task of drawing up regulations for the legalizition and control of professionalism in their ranks have completed their labours. The main feature of the proposals are that professionalism shall be legalized, without restriction upon the sums to be paip to professional players, but that no player shall be permitted to play as a professional unless he is in bona fide employment. In order to enforce the latter proviso, clubs employing professionals will be required to keep a register of their men so engaged and the occupation which they fellow. Penalties are proposed for the nen- carrying out of the letter and spirit of the rule. Clubs committing a breach of the laws will be liable to a heavy fine, while in the Case of comp,tition clubs so offending, they will also be liable to the deduction of points in the competition table. Players, too, who break the rules will be liable to suspension. It is understood the full scheme will simply be presented to the General Commit- tee and that its adoption will be deferred in order to give members of the committee and the clubs they represent a full opportunity of considering its pro- visions.

The General Committee of the Northern Uniod held their monthly meetine, at Manchester on Tuesday night, undes the presidency of Mr. J. E. Warren, Warrington. —The report of the sub-committee was presented, and unanimously adopted. It embraced stringent rules governing prefess.onalism. A professional is defined as a player who receives remuneration over and above travelling expenses actually paid, and all professionals must be registered. Players may be transferred fronz one club to another. Every professional must be in bona-fide employment, and will not he allowed to pig' Rugby football unless in such employment. The folio ing situations are not to be considered hona-fide emplor meats viz, :—Billiard markers,waitera in licensed houses or any employment in connection witK a club. No pro- fessional is to receive pay of any description during the close season.—lt was rirolved that the counties to take part in next year's championship should be Cheshire. NOTES ON GA.M.ES AND

ATHLETICS.

Caicxrr.

Warrington's tide of ill-fortune turned completely on Saturday, when the premier eleven succeeded in admin- istering a severe defeat to one of their strongest oppon- ents, viz., Liverpool. Ills a good number of years since the Liverpool men met defeat on the Arpley en- closure,and possibly it may be a long time before such a thing happens again. The win was a great surprise to both elevens,for the visitors were well represented and in bowling at any rate bad the assistance of the Lan- cashire and Liverpool amateur, W. B. Stoddart. There was a fair attendance of spectators, to whom the lively afterneon's cricket was most enjoyable.

The Liverpudlians won the toss and elected ta bat on the soft wicket,the general anticipation being that they would he good for 200 at least. Eimondson and Hilton. the first pair,wera opposed by Dr. Smith and Bibby. The " Dr." found the wicket to his liking and dismissed Ednaondson in the first over. From this point Warring- ton may be said to have never " looked back." Dr. Smith was in great form and nobody could do anything with his bowling. Wickets went down with regularity, and the innings closed for 48. W. B. Stoddart arrived from Old Trafford in time to he " last man in," but his partner was caught out with the first ball sent dos; n. Dr. Smith's six wickets only cost 15 runs, while Bibby and Thewlis captured a couple each for 24 and 3 respec- tively.

3. Callon and E. Connor commenced the Warrington 'innings, and were opposed by Stoddart and Goodwin. Cnunor drove the former splendidly to the boundary in the first over, but the bowler had his revenge for the batsmen failed to resist the tempting slows, missed the first ball in the second over and was stumped. However Thewlis proved a useful helpmate to Callon and the score rose at a fast rate,'the majority of the runs coming from the amateur. Oldknow relieved Goodwin. and checked the rate of scoring slightly, but Stoddart was despatched le quick succession by both batsmen to the boundary. Thewlis also drove adknow for four, hut Goodwin re- lieved Stoddart at 47 and soon effected a separation, but not before Callen had made the winning bit amidst ringing cheers. At 48 Callon was bowled by Oldknow, and Thewlis was seat back by Goodwin. Both batsmen had given a capital display. and neither gave a chance. Disaster followed fast for Warrington, and sia wickets were down for 51, With L. A. Voisey and G. Clarke associated Stoddart again resumed, and both batsmen, especially the former, caused plenty of amusement in their endeavours to get the slow bowler away. A long sequence of singles were credited to each batsensn' and the partnership resulted in the addition of 39 runs bcfore Clarke was caught, his 20 having been made without blemish. Voisey left soon after, his 22 having been a characteristic innings. W. H. Podmore carried out his bat, the innings closing for 105.

The Warrington batting was deserving of the highest praise, scoring not only being rapid, but the cricket it- self was of a very fair order. Callon, Thewlis, Yoisey, and Clarke, all played a Rue innings. A feature of the gains was the bold way in which the home bats " went tor Steddart." His howling was treated with such scant respect that he only got one wicket for 44 runs. Rather a contrast with his performance earlier in the week of capturing all the Surrey second eleven wickets for a average of about 7 runs each. Oldknow's 4 for 11 was a good performance. I hope Warrington will now follow the path of success they have so auspiciously entered upon.

The second eleven was meanwhile accomplishing doughty deeds at Augburtb, though they were unable to bring the match to any more definite conclusion than a very favourable draw. In batting there was a great improvement shown, th 3 venture realising 145 runs. Silcock and 11. Pierpoint put on 80 runs for the seventh wicket, the former carrying out his bat for a finely- played 60. Pierpoint's 32 was by far his best perform- ance so far. The bowling which got rid of 6 Liverpool men for 85 was also good, Silcook capturing 3 wickets for 39, and Crompton 2 for 20. On the home side Ware- ing took 5 wickets for 56.

CYO ?NO. A racing cyclist named Edwards is creating some commotion in America by riding one hundred miles upon his bicycle every day,wet or fine. He started on January 1 this year,and so far has been successful, inas- much as illness or common sense has not intervened to stop his progress. It is his intention to ride a "century," as 100 miles runs are called in the States,e very day this year of grace, and when 365 " centuries " have been ridden his ambition will be satisfied and, no doubt, his pockets will be full,for riders do no do these things for nothing nowadays. The feat of Edwards, who, by the way,is a Liverpool man,is the outcome of the rage in America for riding " centuries."

The following case bearing upon the rule of the road should interest my readers.—At the Stoke-on-Trent County Court, before his Honour, Judge Jordan, Mr. W. , H. Abberley, solicitor, sued Charles Webberley for 353., ' for damages sustained through the alleged negligence of the defendant in riding a bicycle. The plaintiff stated that on the 17th April be was riding down Trentham- road, Longtm,with a companion. He was riding on the i extreme left of the road and his companion more n the middle. At the bottom of an incline Bolgrave-road con- verges with Trentham-road at. a right angle. It was alleged that the defendant, riding ten miles an hour, turned the angle en the wrong side, and in trying to avoid the plaintiff's companion, whom he first saw, ran into the plaintiff, whose machine was smashed. His Honour, iii deciding the case said the question was whether the defendant was guilty of any negligence. A person either riding or driving was not bound to go on any particular side of the road if there was anything in his way. In this case the defendant saw that if he turned in a particular way and kept too much to his own side he would have run down the other bicyclist, and therefore he did that which he had a fright to do with a view to avoiding an accident—he turned.sharoly to the other side. He did not avoid an :accident, but in his (the Judge') opinion he had not been guilty of negli- gence. Verdict liar the defendant without costs. When a tyre goes down without apparent reason, it is advisable to see that the valve is working all right. A bit of grit may have got in, or the rubber may have perished. Now. an easy way to ascertain whether all is right in the valve department is—turn your wheel until the valve points straight downward, then take a narrow , tumbler or wineglass, fill it with water, and immerse the valve therein ; if bubbles rise, the escape of air is at the valve, and it will be necessary to take it out and examine it. Should it be of the Woods variety, very often a new valve tube will put things to rights ; if a Lucas, pay particular attention to the minute leather washer on to which the valve stem beds.

What with the recent Whitsun and two days' Glasgow meetings, &c., several of the leading, professional have no need to grumble at the share of the prize money they have annexed during the last four weeks. The following cradka have scooped in these snug little amounts at the two last meetings at which they com- peted: Chinn, £l.OO ; Meyer, £5O ;T. Osborn and Camp. £3O each ; nd Gascoyne, £2O. Not bad for one or two afternoons' work.

-- " Cycler's News " this week publishes a portrait or Mr. Ae.Lon Hughes, and remarks, "A hard rider and a good worker b Mr. Acton Hughes, the hon. secretary of the rapidly rising Latchford C.C., and it goes without easing that he is very popular in Warrington cycling eiretes. Mr. anghes is in" the trade," his works being situated at Papicy, where be turns out the,Record cycles. He holds the Teiegraph, K, and Penegrine agencies, and being such a well-known and popular man, it is not surprising to hear that he is doing well in the " wire " town."
