TliE EXAMINER. FRIDAY„TUNE 10 Ib9B

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S PER- MANENT BENEFIT BUILDING SOCIETY. Three addresses were given in Parr Hall on Monday at services in connection with the Salvation Army by ," General " Booth. " Colonel" Lawley was preeent at each of the services, and led the prayers and hinging. There was a rather better attendance in the afternoon than in the morning. " General " Booth, who was accorded a very hearty weleome, delivered the principal address. He spoke of the good meeting that had • been gr and said he always found tnat at either on Sundays or week-days a poor sheerlvdic in esthheelmdo er meeting was made into a good one if they could get some- one to the penitent form. There had been lately a good deal of commotion in the Church of England, amongst the bishops, clergy and laity, upon the question, among others, as to whether candles should be lighted in the churches and mass said, as in the Catholic Church. He (General Booth) recommended them all to try the peni- tent bench. eApplausen He did not mind if they burned a dozen candles. (I. %tighter.) He did not , quarrel with the Cetholice for having in their churches a good picture of the Lord Jesus Christ, or having a handsome cross, if they only made good men, sanctified and filled them with the Holy Ghost and sent them out to try and save sinners. (Aeplause.) But whether the world got more godly, more like Jesus Christ, more full of pity for the penitent man and woman,more taken up and occupied with the honour of Jehovatethe glory of God,rnaking His power known and His name honoured,he very much doubted. Turning to his Bible the General selected the following text, " 0 Lord,what shall I say when Israel turneth their becks before their enemies ?"—Joehue yin. 7. He described Joshua as the greatest and purest character in the book. He was a soldier. He fought. He (the speaker) liked him for that. He fought, an he won. After reciting the scriptural story connected with the words of the text, General Booth spoke of the failures and successes of individual religion, and gave what he considered was the best means for preventing the former and attaining the latter. Another service was held in the evening. THE MEETING FROM A SPECTATOR'S POINT OF VIEW. On Monday Warrington in general and the Salvation Army in particular had the pleasure of a visit from General Booth. That a visit from him is an event in Salvation Army circles no one can deny. The (lames- MODS which are given utterance to, testify to the venera- tion and respect in which the General is held by his fol- lowers. The writer who saw him for the first timer on Monday, was more impressed by the character of the leader than by the meeting itself. General Booth's is the type of face one expects to see on a leader of men. The face is somewhat stern in aspect though brightened at times by a kindly smile, and it is fittingly framed with a beard and curly grey locks. It is unmistakably a face bearing the stamp of individuality. One look in the kindly face of the " general" will dispel any doubts one might have as to the earnestness of his ; purpose. The earnestness and fervour with which each ' officer and follower throws himse f into the meeting or work is more akin to the style of the fiery and turbulent Asiatic than a Eurepean in religious maters. From an ordinary spectator's point of view, a Salvation Army meeting—particularly when a high officer is present—is highly interesting, especially from the way in which it ,is carried on. The stage is occupied by the speaker and a band while at the foot of it is the " penitence " table, where those who are conscious of their own short- comings and have a desire for repentance are led. The meeting opens with a prayer from one of the officers, everyone throwing himself or herself wbole-heartedly Into the praise of God. No particular form of prayer is observed, but of its fervour and power over listeners there is no room far doubt, the speaker frequently having to pause through the ex- pressions wrung from his audience. This may continue for about twenty minute!, when someone from the plat- form sings a hymn, which the audience supply a chorus to. Other speeches, songs and prayers follow, until the great event,the " General's" sermon Comes on Though the" General "at the meeting en Monday tole a text, , he didn't confine himself in any way to the limits of the subject. He illustrate] his points by short stories from his experiences in the States,also enlivening his remarks by flashes of dry humour. Another prayer follows and whilst the exhortations of the" General " are being re- sponded to by exelimations of every description from all parts of the hall, individuals rise from where they were kneeling and proceed to the peniteece form. The officers and "lasses' in the meantime are rimeing about the ball begging and praying to those they thought likely, and strangers in particular to come to the " peni- tence" table. The writer and a friend were tackled at five different time'. Our questioners had no false senti- ment in their nature and promptly told us we were full of sin and fit subjects for confession and repentance. No ens is supposed to leave the meeting whilst this in pro- gress. Should they want to leave they are expected to I go during the sineinge Though the modus operandi of the Salvatieniste may, at times, present an appearance of incongruity to the stranger accustomed to quieter demonstrations of Christianity, one is forcibly impressed by the earnestness and sincerity of purpose whicie actuates all enrolled under the banner of the Army, and leaves the meeting with a conviction that it is a power in the land for the working of good and the mitigation of evil.

GENERAL BOOTH AT WARRING'

There was a fair attendance as:, the annual general meting of members of this society, held in GoWarne- street Schoolroom on Monday evening for the purpose .of declaring a dividend, electing managers, auditors and surveyors, and conducting the 'ordinary business of the society. Mr. :;ouchier presided, and amongst those pre- sent were :—Messrs. R. Isherwood, James Hurdin,g, E. Fenton, J. Baxter, John Orealy, raeorge Hanksy, with Mr. Josiah Longland (solicitor), and Mr. Peter Dalton (secretary). The CHMIMAN, in opening the proceedings, said he was very sorry they could not congratulate themselves upon a bett,r attendance. He would like to draw their attentioa t) the progress that had been made during the past two or three years. They were aware that the Society received an official intimation two years ago that an alteration had been made with respect to the payment of dividends from prospective interest, and that they were unable to declare a dividend in conse- quence. It was pointed out at the last annual meeting that the prcepeative interest would provide a dividend of itself in future almost without any business during the year. It would to a certain extent. This year they brought forward the money to be paid from the pet dividends which they were not allowed to touch last rear, and so it would be throughout tbe history a the sJciety. Four per cent. was a satislactery dividend tut whilst looking upon it as satisfactory they should not forget that the payment of a dividend in a society like that was not the sole ob- ject in view. The object was to teach members thrift, and help them to get a house of their own. He knew of many instances where members had been able to buy tileir own house through the aid of the society. The rats

of payment was exceedingly low. Thee I‘. as not another seciety in the town where the interest to be repaid was as low as in theirs. If they took the trouble to work out the interest which was paid they would find that it was not more than 4 1-8 per cent. There was no society in the town whose they cuula get money lane under similar circumstances at less than five per cent, (Ap- plause.) The SECRETARY (Ml. Dalton), than read the annual re- port and statement of accounts which etated that the manager had pleasure in laying the Twenty-eighth annual statement of accounts before the shareholders. During the year, the sum of £1,009 173 61. had been re- ceived as subscriptions, £2,003 53. 6J. for Repayments and laterest, and £lO 19s. 101. entrance fees, fines, Am, making the total receipts, £3,024 23. 10d. The working expenses were £6B 4s BJ. Tne withdrawals amounted to £1,261 ss. 6J.—Ths sum of £2,222 2s. lid. had been advanc,d to members There were 208 subscribers hold- ing 2745 shares, and 41 borrowers holding 1824 shares making 249 members holding 457 shares. Tut, bonuse this year amounted to £367 10s. on the amount lent, 01 which sum £674s is divided as profit, and £3OO 63 cars tied to the prixrective interest account. Under the Build- ing Societies' Act, 1894, the Accounts of all Building Societies required to be made up in such a form and to centain such particulars as the Chief Registrar of Friendly Societies may, with the approval of the Secretary of State, direct. The form of account which has been pre- scribed by the Chief Registrar requires that the balance ot capital remaining due upon the mortgage securities should la stated separately from the prospective interest thereon, in other words the balance of the £B2 10+ (the net amount advanced per £100) remaining unpaid is to be distinguished from the balance or the £l7 103 (the bonus charged) also remaining unpaid. The accounts of the society for the past year have been made up on this basis and work out as follows :—Total amount remaining owing upon mortgages £10,099 63 Id, of which only £3921 153 id can be regarded as capital, the balance of £1177 ha representing the proportionate amount of in- tercet thereon, calculated for the number of years the several mortgages have still to run. The profits for the year, including £6 la 6J, from last account, after de- ducting working expo, ens, amount to £344 17s 7d. out of which the managers recommend a dividend of 4 per cent, which will take £316 33 7d, leaving a balance of £2B 143, which they direct to be carried forward to next year's account. The whole of the managers retire. Messrs. Harding, Wallington, Fenton, Isherwood, Bouchier, and Baxter are eligible and offer themselves for re-election. Six additiooal managers will he taken from the Register in rotation. The CHAIRMAN said he would like the Secretary to tell them the amount of money in the bank at the present

time. The SECRETARY said £215 was in hand at the bank and in addition over £BO had been taken that night. The CHAIRMAN said the members would see that al- though the balance sheet stated that there was a bal- ance owing to the bank, the society had now a largp I amount in the hands of the bankers. They would also notice that the working expenses were also very small, being only £6B 43. Bi. Be did not think anyone w3uld say that that was excessive. Although there had been a ' run on the society during the past two years, they had ' been able to pay off all the applicants and still have a largo amount amount in hand. He thought that was something they ougnt to congratulate themselves upon. The society was never in a sounder position. If it were I decided to vol ntarily wind up the society, they would be able to pay 20s. in the £, and also be able to pay the interest to the shareholders. He did not think there were many societies in that position. Mr. ENGLAND moved the adopticn of the report and bilsnce sheet. Ile thought they ought to congratulate the riaaneg-rs and officiala for the able manner in which they had conducted the society.lle was surprised that the general public did not take more advantage of the bene- fits the society offered. John E. Wright end William Boille, jun., were re-appoin- ted surveyors. The auditors appoluted were Messrs. A. W. Howard and George Armstrong. They had bad a satisfactory year and a very satisfactory balance sheet. WATER POLO. WARRINGTON v. SEF Oa Tuesday evening at the Legh-street baths,Warring- ton in the Liverpool and District Water Polo competition. Warrington and Sefton (first teams) played off their fix- ture. The local team took the shallow end and Salton the deep end. In very short time a foul was given against Locker the Warriugton captain, but he shortly after- wards swam with the ball well up to the goal mouth and shot straight in. The Sefton custodian promptly handed it out,and A. Ashton as promptly returned rt scoring the first goal. Beaman sent in a stinging shot which scored again. Some goad play followed Sefton passing well, and Spratt scored a rather doubtful goal for the visiters. On reigimption of play Warrington pressed hard to score, rapid passing from Beaman to Creighton and across to W. Ashton resulting in another goal. 04utinuing to press Warrington again scored, this time Locker doing tho trick. Some good play by Sefton took the ball dangerously near to the Warrington goal and a shot WAS made for goal, but Creightcn anticipated it. Boscow's Play was prominent at this juncture but half time Was Ailed. Warrington now took the deep end and some good play by A. Ashton was ineffectual, the Setton men fooling repeatedly. In retaliation at this stage Locker fouled and was ordered out of the water. This might have had a strong influence on the game but matters were equalised through one of the Sefton men taking cratnP and being obliged to leave the bath. Sefton n got the ball and took it well forward and despite 505 cow's endeavours the ball was shot in, ant, a splendid save t'y Robertson the Warrington custodian was the re- sult. The time shortly after expired leaving the result Warrington 4 goals, Sefton 1 goal. WARRINGTON RESERVE V. SEFTON 2sn. A friendly contest between these second teams was shortly afterwards proceeded with. Warrington hid the shallow end to commence with. Sefton got the halt first, and in a struggle for the ball between Hampton and a Sefton man, a foul was given against Sefton, but Hampton failed to score. Later Warrington were again unfortunate in not scoring the ball striking the top bar. A shot in the Sefton goal by Hampton came near scor- ing. Sefton now tried reprisals and Knowles got a de- serving goal for them,though the Warrington custodian Bailey made a good attempt to save. Continued pres- sure had its result in another goal for Sefton by Bowes. Warrington making a combined effort the ball was taken for ward,and Hughes securing it, not being opposed just then by a Sefton man ,swam close into the goal and very deliberately shot it through. The game ended with the result Sefton 2 val.,. Warrington 1 goal. 100 YARDS CLUB HANDICAP. Before the commencement of the polo matches a 100 yards club handicap was swam off in which there were some seven or eight competitors. The finish was very close, two f the competitors being to ordinary view a dead heat. The result was given A. Ashton first,Robert son second, and Hamilton third.

"MUCH ADO ABOUT NOTHING."

The Chairman moved the confirmation of the minutes Of the previous Council meeting.—Mr. Gilmour referred to the minute respecting the appointment of Mr. Gaskia as consulting engineer to the Water Committee, and said he wished to give his reasons why he voted against that resolution at the previous meeting of the Council.—The Chairman : We cannot revise the proceedings Of a previous Council meeting. The question now is whether the minutes are a correct record of the proceedings.—Mr. Gilmour: I can give my reasons why I voted against the resnlmieo.—The Chairman That would be irregu- lar.—Mr. Gilmour : No sir ; it is no use coming here if we c-nnot cliscuss the minutes of the last mecting.—l hive a right, I contend, to state why I voted against the resolution—The Chairman: The proposition before the meeting is the confirmation of the minutes. Are they correzt or incorrect P—Mr. Gilmour : How can the public know why I voted against !the reaolution unless I am allowed to tell them P—The Chairman : We don't want to again go over the same arguments brought for- ward at the last meeting.—Mr. Gilmour : I have a right to sneak on these minutes.—The Chairman you can speak as to whether they are a correct record or not. I am in the hands of the Council. The resolution before us is cch‘ Cher the minutes are a correct record of the proceedings.—Mr. Gilmour : It cannot be a correct record unless the names of the three members, who voted against the resolution are stated.—The Chairman : You contend the minutes are not correct because they do not give the names of those who opposed one of the resolution& 2—Mr.Stone : Mr. Gilmour complains that his name is not given as having , voted against the resolution; he will find that it is given on name 45--Mr.Gilmour : I contend I have a perfect right to explain my views as to why I voted that way. The public don't know.—The Chairman: At the last Council meeting you had full opportunity of telling the public through the press of what your views were on the subject..--..Mr.Gilmour : These are the minutes which were passed an committee. Now is my opportunity for speaking.—Mr Emmett: He has a perfect right to speak on any minute.—Mr. Stone: The minutes were confirmed at the last meeting of the Council, and Mr. I Gilmour had his opportunity then.—Mr. Gilmour : If you read the reports of other Councils you will see they discuss the minutes.—The Chairman : The minutes were confirmed at the last meeting.—Mr.Gihnour : Why have they come before us P—The Chairman: It is the usual procedure —Mr. Gilmour: Will you allow me to explain why I didn't vote for the resolution? —The Chairman : If you chose to shut your mouth at the time,you cannot open it now.—Mr. Gilmour : Par- liamentary languege,Mr. Chairman.—Mr. Ormond: Is it customary to record tae names of every person who voted for or against any resolution in pommittee.—The Chairman : No.—Mr. Ormand : I think Mr. Gilmour will appreciate the po3nt.—Mr. Gilmour: I don't arr. predate it. DEATH OF A WARRINGTON MAN AT

BLACKPOOL. VERDIC I' OF MANSLAUGEITER.

Mr. Parker, the district coroner, resumed his inquiry at HI ckpool on Tu-s lay into the death of a labourer, Patrick Meakin, of Warrington, 47, found dead with a wound in his head at his lodgings last week. Agnes Wright, 79, Ribble-row, said deceased lodged with her. About Biz o'clock p.m. on May 28th ho was brought home by another man. Be appeared to be very drunk, and could scarcely stand. Deceased sat down in a chair, looking ghastly. Meakin did not move from the chair until uke o'clock, when witness asked him to go to bed,but he said he would keep the house for her while she went out. When witness returned she noticed that deceased had vomited. At 5.15 next morning wit- ness was awakened by a man named Mclntyre, who shouted out : "Misius, get up. Pat's dead." She found Wallin lying on the kitthen floor near a chair, where she had left rum the previous night. Deceased had lodged with her tor about 15 weeks, having come from Warrington. Witness had received a letter from de- ceased's wife,who was in Ireland. Meakin had always enjoyed good health, and w 'tress had noticed an injury to his head when she went to bed. He did not make any complaint, as to anyone having injured him. John M'lutyre ccrroborated,atter which James O'Neil, who was in the vicinity of the Royal Hotel vaults on the afternoon of the day named, was called. He spoke to seeing deceased enter the vaults of the Royal Hotel in the a.ternoon of May 28th, In a short time he came tumtling out, and fell on his head. A man in an apron came and picked him up. Two men afterwards took him away.

Frank bu .kett, 41, Seed-stre.t, Blackpool, said Heat about 530 on the day in question he was in the Itlyal Hotel vaults. Lie was standing at the bar, oppoaite the decemed when he came into the vaulta, fits attention was drawn by a noise behind him, and upon turning round he saw the barman take hold of a man whom he was putting out. Ile took him to the dour and gave him a pitch with his two hands, and deceased fell on his head on the ground. The barman returned in about a minute, and said to the manager, " I have not struck Min, I think he h 8 had enough." It was shown by further evidence that deceased, after being refused drink by three barmen, refused to go out, and when Gamble (the barman) put Meakin out he struggled and fought all the way. Aftr evidence of a similar nature had been given, Dr.Lvonard Molloy was Called ,and said he made a post mortem examination of the deceased. There were nO. marks on hini except a Scalp wound on the right sie. 0' the Lead and a small bruise on the outer eyelid of th right eye.oa examining the wound on the head he limn a large clot of blood. There had been some er ern bleeding, and the injury was such as he would ha 77 expected Cu a man whe bad fallen or been thrown dim I. There was no blc.od from the eye. THE ASSAULT ON A WARRINGTON BAILIFF AT EARLESTOWN.

DISCHARGE OF THE MALE PRISONER. Michael Knight, 48, collier, and Margaret Knight, 39, husband and wife, were together charged at the Liver- Doel Quarter Sessions on Tuesday with having, at New- ton-in-Makeifield, on the 13ch of last month, wounded Walter Newton, an assistant bailiff of the Warrington County Court. Mr. Rigby Swift was counsel for the prosecution, and prisoners were defended by Mr. R 039 Brown. It was stated on behalf of the case for the prosecution that on the date in question Newton arrested the male prisoner on a warrant in Leigh-street, Earles- town. Prisoner endeavoured to break away, and whilst the prosecutor was struggling with him the female pm soner struck him (prosecutor) several times in the face. A crowd collted,and the prosecutor was kickcd from behind and fell to the ground. He was, it was alleged, seriously maltreated, and while he was on the ground the male prisoner, it was stated, kicked him in the side, breaking a rib,a portion of which penetrated the lung. the injuries thus received were dangerous, and the pro. secutor had not yet fully recovered.—The defence was that the injuries received by the prosecutor was no. caused by the prisoners,hut by someone in the crowd that had collected.—The jury found the male prisonert not guilty, and he was di9charged.—Margaret Knighu was found guilty of a commou assault,and sent to priao for a mmtb.

GAS 'MANAGER'S REPORT.

Mr. Swann in his report stated that in the course of a week or so the stock of coke would all be sold, and he should therefore be pleased to know if the price of coke was to remain as at present. The gas main in Haydock- street would require extending about 100 yards, go that the cottages recently erected there might be supplied with gas. They had as many pipes in stock as would be required for the extension. The pressure on the water Mains, at three diffn.ent parts of the District was tried with the following results :—Wargrave-road, 25th per square inch ; Esrl-strest. 17lb per square inch ; St. Helens-road, 71b per square inch. Under these circum- stances a 41n, pipe would give an ample supply of water to the new street off Wargrave-road, in which M ssrs Fl zgerald were building new cottages. Of course in a few years the 4 inch pipes which at present supplied the Warg,rave district would have to be replsced with larger ones, but before that is done there should be SoMe arrangements made for giving a better supply of water at toe higher parts of the District, callers at present it is very inadequate. The hydrants had been tested and they were in good working condition. The consumption of water during the month as per registering of the station meters was as follows:—Newton Bridge meter, 3,720,000 gallons ; Viaduct Works meter, 2,180,000 !gallons ; Park- side meter, 12,000 gallons ; total, 5.912,000 gallons. Cor- responding month last year. 4,735,000 gallons ; increase, 1,177,000 gallons.—The committee ordered that the price of coke at the Gas Works be left in the hands of the Chairman, the Clerk, and the Gas Mana- ger ; that the Gas Manager lay a 4-inch water main in Rydal-street, cff Wargrave-road ; a street lamp lighting and extinguis,aing time table was submitted to the cemmittee which after amendment was agreed to. It was ordered that the Clerk report at tile next meeting of the committee the position of the Council under the Electric Lighting Acts of 1882 and 1889, and to make any recommendations thereon which he may consider desirable ; that Mr. Borron, Mr. Ormand, and Mr. Beatty be a sub-committee to go into the matter of Messrs. Fitzgerald's " extras " in respect to their account for the new purifying house. At a further meeting of the committee on Monday afternoon (Ju-ne 6th) it was recommended that the ten- der of Messrs. T. R. and W. Barr,of Leeds, for cannel at 11s. 10d.par too be accepted,and also that the tender of Mr. Woods, ot St. Ffelens, for a new retort house at the gas works at £682 he accepted. The Chairman moved the confirmation of the minutes. Mr. Gilmour objected to the inclusion of the minutes passed that afternoon declaring that it was an infringe- ment of the Standing Orders as no notice had been given of it.

The Chairman : There is no infringement that I am aware of,

The minutes were confirmed, Mr. Gilmour only vot- ing against._

HIGHWAYS AND SANITARY COMMITTEE. Mr. Brierley in his report as sanitary inspector said the district was comparatively free from infectious diseases, two cases of enteric fever had however been removed to the Hospital at Warrington. Since his last report 24 notices of sundry nuisances had been issued and were in course of abatement. In the Surveyor's de- partment Mr. Brierley reported that since the last meet- ing the workmen had been engaged cleaning the west outlet tanks, macadamizing Sankey-street and Fair- clough-street, removing old materials from Ashton-road in Sankey-street, as well as relaying flags and repaving tiles in several 'Ames ; general scavenging of the dis- trict, including provision for the fairs, fencing and curb- ing, footpath extension in Wargrave-road ; to be fol- lowed with flagging and tiling up to War- , grave House • repaving in Earle-street and ' Bridor-street, Cleaning out the Central cutfall tanks. _The committee resolved that the plans fur two houses, bakehouse, and stable in Flaydock-street, for Mr. John Clarke ; house near Wargrave rail way bridge for Mr. N. Siddeley ; two houses, Birlev-street, for Messrs. Wilkin- son and Flint ; and alterations to yards and closets at !four houses, Rob-lane, for Mr. G. Webb, be approved and passed.—The Clerk read correspondence baying re- ference to the maintenance and treatment of small-pox cases, (a) from the Warrington Corporation, (4) Wigan Corporation, and (c) Leigh Joint flospitrl Board. THE FRENCH CHAMBER.

PARIS, THURSDAY. M. Paul Doschanal was to-day permanently elected President of the Chatabor of Deputies by 287 votes, against 277 given to N. Brisson. THE OPEN GOLF CHAMPIONSHIP. Harry Vardon, of Scarborough, won the open golf champtonanip at Prestwick yesterday. CRICKET.

The Yorkshire cricket &even bad an easy task in play- ing against Hampshire yesterday,. Going in first they scored 228,and then dismissed the Southern team for the wretched total of 45. Wainviright had the remarkable analysis of sewn wickets for 24 runs. Hampshire fol- lowed on 181 behind,and before stumps were pulled up scored ten without loss. There was some good scoring at Bristol in the match between Gloucestershire and N Ars. The home side were bitting all day,and made 321 for three wickets. Sewell made 108.

At Leyton, Essex playing against W'arwicksbire, were at the wickets all day, and made 307 for nice. Kent secured a considerable advantage in the first day's play against Sussex at Catford. Sussex made 151. Kent then scored 103 without loss. A weak M.C.C. team was completely outplayed by Derbyshire at Lord's. The comity dismissed the club eleven for 94, and then scored 246 for 6 wickets. A match between Cambridge University and Surrey began at Cambridge. Surrey scored 136. The Light Blues made 65 for five wickets. THE LEYLAND DISASTER—INQUEST AND VERDICT. The inquest on the bodies of the victims of the Rail. way disaster at Leyland was held yesterday. and 00 jury, after deliberating for a little over half an hour, re- . turned into court with a unanimous verdict to the effect , that the deaths of Ellen Halpin and William Alfred Hick- lin were doe t injuries received in the collision, and that the collision was caused through the gross and culp- able negligence of Joseph Whyte, the driver of the No. 14 train. Whyte was thereupon formally committed by the Coroner to take his trial at the next Manchester Assizes on a charge of manelauohter.—Whyte : Well, gentlemen, I beg to state that I have been dealt with .wrong ; I am innocent.—Mr. Chorlton applied for bail, which was granted, IWhyte being bound over in £lOO. with two sureties of £5O

SCENE IN COURT.

At the Newton Patty Sessions on Thursday before Mr. T. J. Gillespie In the chair) and Colo el Wood, Thomas fieulden and William Houlden, both 0 Westhoughton, near Wigan were summoned for assaulting Arthur Lee, of Eirleetown, on the 31st May.— Moines flouldrin was also summoned for assaulting James Leo on the 4th June. Mr. Knowles said his clients were natives of ,Etries- town and had lived there for some considerable time. They were a father and three seas. The disturbance took place on Whit-Monday about 4 15 in the aftersoo.n. Arthur Lee in company with a man named George Itice went into the Wellington Hotel, Earlestown, for a drink. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EA.RLESTOWN.

[BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT].

The turn out in connection with the May Queen festival, was, as compared with previous displays, of a rather poor description, both in point of num- bers and in quality. The number of character representations was small, and it was freely re- marked that the demonstration was much behind others in point of interest. The attendance of the general public was also sparse, and consequen•ly the financial result will not be so satisfactory as the Committee would wish. The inclemency of the weather on the previous Saturday when the festi- val should have been held, and which entailed an enforced postponement, has doubtless had some- thing to do with the partial failure of the affair. It is hoped, however, that the finances are not quite so bad as is reported.

Affairs at Haydock in connection with the Church do not seem to improve very much. The Vicar called a Vestry Meeting last week for the purpose of completing the business which was so abruptly terminated at the first vestry meeting, and there was a large muster in response to his summons. Beyond throwing plenty of mud, nothing at all was done, and they appear to be in something the some position as they were before calling the meeting. Mr. John Robinson, who has evidently had enough of the squabble, declined to accept the position of parishioner's warden, and the Vicar objected to the nomination of Mr. Hilton upon'a technical ground that he was not a communicant at the Church. He insisted ill pn his objection, and the meeting had to be again adjourned in order that the objeetion might not be legally tested.

I hear that Mr. Young, the station master, will retire from the service of the Railway Company at the end of the present month. Mr. Young is an old and tried servant, and although he may not always have done everything in the way the general public desired, still he has done his duty to the satisfac- tion of his employers, and I hope he will obtain the reward which his labours merited.

Miqß Margaret Gertrude Annie Millais, the original of the famous picture " Marguerite," by the late Sir John Everett Millais, has been married at Rowledgo. Surrey, o Mr.E.A. Parkinson, son of the late Mr.J.Parkinson,of the 18th Hussars.

THE EXAMINER. JUNE 10th, 1895.

FOR some time past the quese Mercantile Maiine • and the Navy. tion has occupied the atten" tion of members of the NavY League, and of others, how to provide means 1) which respectable boys can enter the mercantile marine. It appears to be patent to everybody, except the shipowners, that the increase of the foreign element in the merchant service is a source of weakness, and of possible danger to our naval supremacy. It is but natural that we should look to the merchant service to supply a reserve of sea- men for the defence of our shores against invasion , Yet we shall look in vain if, when the critical mo" , merit comes, it is found to be the case that the crews of the ships sailing under the British flag are aliens, and possibly enemies. How far the ; mischief is capable of cure is not easy to deter" mine. With the shipowners it is a question Of ' 1 wages, and perhaps of discipline too. Foreigners will work for less money, or at least are contest with wages which Englishmen accept unwillingly, and they do not grumble so loud and so long at bad food and, indifferent accommodation. ADr how, for these or other reasons many masters ce,Pl' tainly ship foreigners, with the result that an creasing percentage of them can be found in British vessels.lt may be that this is not the whole. of the explanation, and that the foreign owes his position to a dearth of English seamen. If the latter be the fact, it is not more reassuring than the assumption that tho foreigner is preferred because he is foreign. There ought not to be a dearth of English seamen, either for the QuEsN'S or for private service. The old sea-loving spirit has not died out. For British boys the sea has to' day the some fascination it always had, and if there be a dearth of seamen it is because there ie practically no way open for many boys who would , eagerly embrace a seafaring life. I The result of recent enquiries as to means by which these boys can outer the mercantile service, shows that the only way is to find a firm of ship- owners willing to take apprentices, and then to ' pay a heavy premium and provide an expensive 1 , outfit. The initial cost varies, but is not likely 1 to be less than £5O, especially if the parents or guardians of the boy know nothing about it, and have no friend who does know, to advise them. An outlay of this kind is a bar to the hopes of most boys, end from those apprenticed under these con- ditions the officers of the mercantile service are , 4111 largely recruited, and apparently the supply is - equal to the demand. The want that is mostly ex- perienced,however,is for means by which boys who do not possess the advantages of money or of influence, may nevertheless acquire such a know- ledge of seamanship as will fit them for life before the mast, and enable the country to maintain the supply of native-born sailors. To some extent the reformatory and industrial training ships supply the need ; but it is pertinently urged that there ought to be a way open to boys to enter upon a seafaring career without the preliminary condi- tion of having previously "gone wrong." A gentleman who had apparently given the matter special consideration and study, suggested not long since the formation of " a patriotic institu- tion," supported by the Government and assisted by shipowners, with suitable training ships, and agencies for giving information in all large centres of population. The suggestion is by no means one to be lightly valued, and it should not be too much to hope, in discussing the cognate question of the manning of the navy, that atten- tion may also be given to the supply of the seamen who constitute the National Reserve.
